Steps to reproduce:

```python
import unittest
from moto import mock_lambda
import boto3


@mock_lambda
class TestBase(unittest.TestCase):
    def setUp(self) -> None:
        self.env_var = {}
        self.session = boto3.Session(
            aws_access_key_id="test",
            aws_secret_access_key="test",
            aws_session_token="test",
        )
        return super().setUp()


@mock_lambda
class TestMotoBug(TestBase):
    def test_its_called_with_function_name(self):  # This passes correctly
        self.session.client("lambda").publish_layer_version(
            LayerName="ExampleLayer",
            Content={"ZipFile": b"example-zip"},
        )
        assert (
            "ExampleLayer"
            == self.session.client("lambda").list_layers()["Layers"][0]["LayerName"]
        )
        self.session.client("lambda").delete_layer_version(
            LayerName="ExampleLayer", VersionNumber=1
        )
        assert len(self.session.client("lambda").list_layers()["Layers"]) == 0

    def test_its_called_with_arn(self):  # This one raises a UnrecognizedClientException
        self.session.client("lambda").publish_layer_version(
            LayerName="ExampleLayer",
            Content={"ZipFile": b"example-zip"},
        )
        assert (
            "ExampleLayer"
            == self.session.client("lambda").list_layers()["Layers"][0]["LayerName"]
        )
        self.session.client("lambda").delete_layer_version(
            LayerName=self.session.client("lambda").list_layers()["Layers"][0][
                "LayerArn"
            ],
            VersionNumber=1,
        )
        assert len(self.session.client("lambda").list_layers()["Layers"]) == 0

```

Expected Behaviour: Both tests should pass according to https://docs.aws.amazon.com/lambda/latest/dg/API_DeleteLayerVersion.html


System Data:

Python: 3.10.10
OS: Mac OS 13.4 (22F66)
Moto:  4.1.11

- **Initialization**: The constructor `__init__` should accept a single parameter `account_holder`, a string representing the name of the account holder. Initialize the account balance to 0.0.
- **Deposit**: Implement a method `deposit` that accepts a single parameter `amount`, a float representing the amount to be deposited. Ensure that the deposit amount is positive; otherwise, print an error message "Deposit amount must be positive".
- **Withdraw**: Implement a method `withdraw` that accepts a single parameter `amount`, a float representing the amount to be withdrawn. Ensure that the withdrawal amount is positive and that there are sufficient funds in the account; otherwise, print appropriate error messages "Withdrawal amount must be positive" or "Insufficient funds".
- **Balance Inquiry**: Implement a method `get_balance` that returns the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
account = BankAccount("John Doe")
account.deposit(100.0)
account.withdraw(50.0)
print(account.get_balance())  # Output: 50.0
account.withdraw(60.0)        # Output: Insufficient funds
account.deposit(-10.0)        # Output: Deposit amount must be positive
account.withdraw(-20.0)       # Output: Withdrawal amount must be positive
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_holder):
        """
        Initializes a new bank account with the given account holder's name and a balance of 0.0.
        
        :param account_holder: str, the name of the account holder
        """
        self.account_holder = account_holder
        self.balance = 0.0

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits the specified amount into the account if the amount is positive.
        
        :param amount: float, the amount to be deposited
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws the specified amount from the account if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
        
        :param amount: float, the amount to be withdrawn
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive")

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: float, the current balance
        """
        return self.balance
```<#sep#>A choir director must select a group of singers from among his 6 tenors and 8 basses. <image>

Where <image> represents logical OR and <image> represents logical exclusive OR (XOR), and vi, j are some boolean variables or their negations. Natalia calls the left side of the equation a XNF formula. Each statement in brackets is called a clause, and vi, j are called literals.

In the equation Natalia has, the left side is actually a 2-XNF-2 containing variables x1, x2, ..., xm and their negations. An XNF formula is 2-XNF-2 if:

  1. For each 1 ≤ i ≤ n, ki ≤ 2, i.e. the size of each clause doesn't exceed two. 
  2. Each variable occurs in the formula at most two times (with negation and without negation in total). Please note that it's possible that a variable occurs twice but its negation doesn't occur in any clause (or vice versa). 



Natalia is given a formula of m variables, consisting of n clauses. Please, make sure to check the samples in order to properly understand how the formula looks like.

Natalia is more into fight than theory, so she asked you to tell her the number of answers to this equation. More precisely, you need to find the number of ways to set x1, ..., xm with true and false (out of total of 2m ways) so that the equation is satisfied. Since this number can be extremely large, you need to print the answer modulo 109 + 7.

Please, note that some variable may appear twice in one clause, or not appear in the equation at all (but still, setting it to false or true gives different ways to set variables).
Input

The first line of input contains two integers n and m (1 ≤ n, m ≤ 100 000) — the number of clauses and the number of variables respectively.

The next n lines contain the formula. The i-th of them starts with an integer ki — the number of literals in the i-th clause. It is followed by ki non-zero integers ai, 1, ..., ai, ki. If ai, j > 0 then vi, j is xai, j otherwise it's negation of x - ai, j (1 ≤ ki ≤ 2,  - m ≤ ai, j ≤ m, ai, j ≠ 0).

Output

Print the answer modulo 1 000 000 007 (109 + 7) in one line.

Map Scale Worksheet Education Com Social Studies Maps Teaching Maps Social Studies Worksheets<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a class hierarchy for a restaurant ordering system. Implement the following classes:

- `MenuItem`: Represents an item on the menu. It should have attributes for `name`, `price`, and `category`.
- `Order`: Represents a customer's order. It should have attributes for the list of `menu_items`, the `customer_name`, and the `order_date`. Implement a method `total_price` that returns the total price of the order.
- `Restaurant`: Represents the restaurant. It should have a list of available `menu_items` and methods for adding a `menu_item`, processing an `order`, and calculating the `total_revenue`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

```python
# Create menu items
burger = MenuItem("Burger", 5.99, "Main Course")
fries = MenuItem("Fries", 2.99, "Appetizer")

# Create a restaurant and add menu items
restaurant = Restaurant()
restaurant.add_menu_item(burger)
restaurant.add_menu_item(fries)

# Create an order
order = Order("Alice", [burger, fries])

# Process the order
restaurant.process_order(order)

# Calculate total revenue
print(restaurant.total_revenue())  # Output: 8.98
```

```python
from datetime import datetime

class MenuItem:
    def __init__(self, name: str, price: float, category: str):
        """
        Initialize a MenuItem with a name, price, and category.
        
        :param name: Name of the menu item.
        :param price: Price of the menu item.
        :param category: Category of the menu item.
        """
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.category = category

    def __repr__(self):
        return f'MenuItem(name={self.name}, price={self.price}, category={self.category})'


class Order:
    def __init__(self, customer_name: str, items: list['MenuItem']):
        """
        Initialize an Order with a customer's name and a list of menu items.
        
        :param customer_name: Name of the customer placing the order.
        :param items: List of MenuItem objects in the order.
        """
        self.customer_name = customer_name
        self.items = items
        self.order_date = datetime.now()

    def total_price(self) -> float:
        """
        Calculate the total price of the order.
        
        :return: Total price of the order.
        """
        return sum(item.price for item in self.items)

    def __repr__(self):
        return f'Order(customer_name={self.customer_name}, items={self.items}, order_date={self.order_date})'


class Restaurant:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initialize a Restaurant with an empty list of menu items and orders.
        """
        self.menu_items = []
        self.orders = []

    def add_menu_item(self, item: MenuItem):
        """
        Add a menu item to the restaurant's menu.
        
        :param item: MenuItem object to be added.
        """
        self.menu_items.append(item)

    def process_order(self, order: Order):
        """
        Process a customer's order.
        
        :param order: Order object to be processed.
        """
        self.orders.append(order)

    def total_revenue(self) -> float:
        """
        Calculate the total revenue from all processed orders.
        
        :return: Total revenue.
        """
        return sum(order.total_price() for order in self.orders)

    def __repr__(self):
        return f'Restaurant(menu_items={self.menu_items}, orders={self.orders})'

When running the PutMetricData API call through a boto mock test when the body is gzip compressed a 404 response is returned from the moto mock which raises a  botocore.errorfactory.ResourceNotFoundException exception. 

Disabling the boto gzip  request compression as outlined in #6692  seems to fix the issue and the post request works fine. 

It seems to be an issue in versions of botocore 1.34.40+ which have a change for the cloudwatch client that just says  that the cloudwatch client was updated to the latest.

https://github.com/boto/botocore/blob/021a6594d3d53e39b6490b02caee0fd8b992a86e/CHANGELOG.rst#L73C5-L73C72

Running the PutMetricData without moto ( directly to AWS ap-southeast-2 ) seems to work fine as well. 

**Testing code**

```
@mock_aws
def test_metric_put(monkeypatch):

    monkeypatch.setenv("AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID", "testing")
    monkeypatch.setenv("AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY", "testing")
    monkeypatch.setenv("AWS_SESSION_TOKEN", "testing")
    monkeypatch.setenv("AWS_SECURITY_TOKEN", "testing")
    monkeypatch.setenv("AWS_REGION", "ap-southeast-2")
    monkeypatch.delenv("AWS_DEFAULT_REGION", raising=False)
    monkeypatch.delenv("AWS_PROFILE", raising=False)

    cloudwatch = boto3.client("cloudwatch")

    metrics = []
    metric_dimensions = [{"Name": "Environment", "Value": "env1"}]
    metric_args = {"Unit": "Seconds", "MetricName": "TestCWMetrics"}

    for type, licences in {
        k: v
        for k, v in {"userTypes": [
             {"id": f"UserType{x}", "expiration": datetime.datetime.now().timestamp() } for x in range(0, 30)
       ]}.items()
        if k in ["userTypes", "appBundles", "apps", "extensions", "portalCapabilities"]
    }.items():
        for licence in licences:
            metrics.append(
                {
                    **metric_args,
                    "Dimensions": [
                        *metric_dimensions,
                        {"Name": "Type", "Value": f"{type}/{licence['id']}"},
                    ],
                    "Value": int((datetime.datetime.fromtimestamp(int(licence["expiration"]) / 1000 ) - datetime.datetime.now()).total_seconds()),
                }
            )

Time - Online activity about solving a problem of time.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class that simulates basic bank account operations. The class should support the following functionalities:

1. **Initialization**: The constructor should accept the account holder's name, account number, and an optional initial balance (default is 0).
2. **Deposit**: A method to deposit a specified amount into the account. The amount must be positive; otherwise, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Deposit amount must be positive".
3. **Withdraw**: A method to withdraw a specified amount from the account. The amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance; otherwise, raise a `ValueError` with the message "Insufficient funds" or "Withdrawal amount must be positive".
4. **Check Balance**: A method to return the current balance of the account.
5. **Display Details**: A method to return a string containing the account holder's name, account number, and current balance.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Input: `account = BankAccount("John Doe", "123456789", 1000)`
- Output: `account.display_account_details()` should return `"Account Holder: John Doe\nAccount Number: 123456789\nBalance: 1000"`
- Input: `account.deposit(500)`
- Output: `account.check_balance()` should return `1500`
- Input: `account.withdraw(200)`
- Output: `account.display_account_details()` should return `"Account Holder: John Doe\nAccount Number: 123456789\nBalance: 1300"`
- Input: `account.withdraw(1500)` should raise a `ValueError` with the message "Insufficient funds"

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_holder_name, account_number, initial_balance=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account.

        :param account_holder_name: The name of the account holder.
        :param account_number: The account number.
        :param initial_balance: The initial balance of the account (default is 0).
        """
        self.account_holder_name = account_holder_name
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: The amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.

        :param amount: The amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is not positive or exceeds the current balance.
        """
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds")
        elif amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive")
        else:
            self.balance -= amount

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Check the current balance of the account.

        :return: The current balance.
        """
        return self.balance

    def display_account_details(self):
        """
        Display the account details.

        :return: A string containing the account holder's name, account number, and current balance.
        """
        return f"Account Holder: {self.account_holder_name}\nAccount Number: {self.account_number}\nBalance: {self.balance}"

## Symptom

Not all methods in mock_databrew work when Dataset has spaces in the name. For example, list_datasets works after I run create_dataset . However, delete_dataset **and** update_dataset do not work after I run create_dataset . I have been using the naming convention for live resources and am able to create and delete them without issue.

## Environment

### Specs
OS: Ubuntu 22.04.3 LTS
Python: 3.10.12
boto3: 1.28.62
botocore: 1.31.62
moto: 4.2.6

### Dockerfile
```Dockerfile
FROM ubuntu:22.04

RUN apt update && DEBIAN_FRONTEND=noninteractive apt install python3 python3-pip -y
RUN /usr/bin/python3 -m pip install botocore boto3 boto moto python-jose -y
```

## Code Snippet

### test.py
```py
from moto import mock_databrew
import boto3

@mock_databrew
def test_delete_dataset():
    databrew_client = boto3.client("databrew")
    stringone = 'testId'
    stringtwo = 'testTable'

    rsp_cre = databrew_client.create_dataset(
        Name=stringone + ' - ' + stringtwo,
        Format='CSV',
        FormatOptions={
            'Csv': {
                'Delimiter': ',',
                'HeaderRow': True
            }
        },
        Input={
            'S3InputDefinition': {
                'Bucket': 'my_import_bucket',
                'Key': stringone + '/import/' + stringtwo + '/data.csv',
            }
        }
    )
    print("=== Create Result ===")
    print(rsp_cre)
    print("")
    
    
    rsp_list = databrew_client.list_datasets()
    print("=== List Result ===")
    print(rsp_list.get("Datasets")[0])
    print("")
    
    rsp_del = databrew_client.delete_dataset(
        Name=rsp_cre.get('Name')
    )
    
    print("=== Delete Result ===")
    print(rsp_del.get('Name'))
    print("")
    
    
test_delete_dataset()

# Merging irregular time series

I have two time series, one being a daily time series and the other one a discrete one. In my case I have share prices and ratings I need to merge but in a way that the merged time series keeps the daily dates according to the stock prices and that the rating is fitted to the daily data by ticker and date. A simple merge command would only look for the exact date and ticker and apply NA to non-fitting cases. But I would like to look for the exact matches and fill the dates between with last rating.

Daily time series:

ticker       date        stock.price
AA US Equity 2004-09-06  1
AA US Equity 2004-09-07  2
AA US Equity 2004-09-08  3
AA US Equity 2004-09-09  4
AA US Equity 2004-09-10  5
AA US Equity 2004-09-11  6

Discrete time series
ticker        date        Rating Last_Rating
AA US Equity   2004-09-08   A         A+
AA US Equity   2004-09-11   AA        A
AAL LN Equity  2005-09-08   BB        BB
AAL LN Equity  2007-09-09   AA        AA-
ABE SM Equity  2006-09-10   AA        AA-
ABE SM Equity  2009-09-11   AA        AA-

Required Output:

ticker       date        stock.price  Rating
AA US Equity 2004-09-06    1             A+
AA US Equity 2004-09-07    2             A+
AA US Equity 2004-09-08    3             A
AA US Equity 2004-09-09    4             A
AA US Equity 2004-09-10    5             A
AA US Equity 2004-09-11    6             AA

I would be very greatful for your help.

-
please make your data easy to load, i.e. dput instead of/in addition to copy-pasting (this is simple rolling merge problem in data.table, but your current data presentation is too cumbersome to work with) – eddi Oct 28 '13 at 20:56

Maybe this is the solution you want. The function na.locf in the time series package zoo can be used to carry values forward (or backward).

library(zoo)
library(plyr)
options(stringsAsFactors=FALSE)

daily_ts=data.frame(
ticker=c('A','A','A','A','B','B','B','B'),
date=c(1,2,3,4,1,2,3,4),
stock.price=c(1.1,1.2,1.3,1.4,4.1,4.2,4.3,4.4)
)
discrete_ts=data.frame(
ticker=c('A','A','B','B'),
date=c(2,4,2,4),
Rating=c('A','AA','BB','BB-'),
Last_Rating=c('A+','A','BB+','BB')
)

res=ddply(
merge(daily_ts,discrete_ts,by=c("ticker","date"),all=TRUE),
"ticker",
function(x)
data.frame(
x[,c("ticker","date","stock.price")],
Rating=na.locf(x\$Rating,na.rm=FALSE),
Last_Rating=na.locf(x\$Last_Rating,na.rm=FALSE,fromLast=TRUE)
)
)

res=within(
res,
Rating<-ifelse(
is.na(Rating),
Last_Rating,Rating
)
)[,setdiff(colnames(res),"Last_Rating")]

res

Gives

#  ticker date stock.price Rating
#1      A    1         1.1     A+
#2      A    2         1.2      A
#3      A    3         1.3      A
#4      A    4         1.4     AA
#5      B    1         4.1    BB+
#6      B    2         4.2     BB
#7      B    3         4.3     BB
#8      B    4         4.4    BB-

The expression consists of non-negative integers, operators (`+`, `-`, `*`, `/`), parentheses `(` and `)`, and spaces. Your task is to evaluate the expression and return the result as a floating-point number. The expression should be evaluated according to the standard operator precedence and parentheses rules. If the expression is invalid, your function should raise a `ValueError`.

**Input:**
- A string `expression` (1 ≤ len(expression) ≤ 100) representing the mathematical expression.

**Output:**
- A floating-point number representing the result of the evaluated expression.

**Sample Input:**
```
"3 + 5 * (2 - 8) / 4"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
-7.0
```

```python
def evaluate_expression(expression):
    """
    Evaluates a mathematical expression provided as a string.
    
    Args:
    expression (str): A string representing the mathematical expression.
    
    Returns:
    float: The result of the evaluated expression.
    
    Raises:
    ValueError: If the expression is invalid.
    """
    def apply_operator(operators, values):
        operator = operators.pop()
        right = values.pop()
        left = values.pop()
        if operator == '+':
            values.append(left + right)
        elif operator == '-':
            values.append(left - right)
        elif operator == '*':
            values.append(left * right)
        elif operator == '/':
            if right == 0:
                raise ValueError("Division by zero")
            values.append(left / right)
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid operator")

    def greater_precedence(op1, op2):
        precedence = {'+': 1, '-': 1, '*': 2, '/': 2}
        return precedence[op1] > precedence[op2]

    expression = expression.replace(' ', '')
    operators = []
    values = []
    i = 0

    while i < len(expression):
        if expression[i] == '(':
            operators.append(expression[i])
        elif expression[i] == ')':
            while operators and operators[-1] != '(':
                apply_operator(operators, values)
            if operators and operators[-1] == '(':
                operators.pop()
            else:
                raise ValueError("Mismatched parentheses")
        elif expression[i] in '+-*/':
            while (operators and operators[-1] != '(' and
                   greater_precedence(operators[-1], expression[i])):
                apply_operator(operators, values)
            operators.append(expression[i])
        elif expression[i].isdigit() or expression[i] == '.':
            j = i
            while j < len(expression) and (expression[j].isdigit() or expression[j] == '.'):
                j += 1
            values.append(float(expression[i:j]))
            i = j - 1
        else:
            raise ValueError("Invalid character in expression")
        i += 1

    while operators:
        op = operators.pop()
        if op == '(' or op == ')':
            raise ValueError("Mismatched parentheses")
        apply_operator(operators, values)

    if len(values) != 1:
        raise ValueError("Invalid expression")

    return values[0]

# Test
expression = "3 + 5 * (2 - 8) / 4"
result = evaluate_expression(expression)
print(result)

</think>To solve the problem of finding the number of valid groups of singers from 6 tenors and 8 basses where the difference between the number of tenors and basses is a multiple of 4, we can use generating functions and roots of unity.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Define the Problem:**
   - We need to count the number of ways to select a group of singers such that the difference between the number of tenors \( t \) and basses \( b \) is a multiple of 4.
   - The generating function for tenors is \( (1 + x)^6 \) and for basses is \( (1 + x)^8 \).

2. **Use Roots of Unity Filter:**
   - We need to extract coefficients where the exponent difference \( t - b \equiv 0 \pmod{4} \).
   - This can be done using the roots of unity filter. Let \( \omega \) be a primitive 4th root of unity, i.e., \( \omega = i \).

3. **Compute Coefficients for Tenors:**
   - For tenors, the generating function is \( (1 + x)^6 \).
   - We need to compute \( T_r \) for \( r = 0, 1, 2, 3 \), where \( T_r \) is the sum of coefficients of \( x^{4k + r} \) in \( (1 + x)^6 \).

   \[
   T_r = \frac{1}{4} \left[ (1 + 1)^6 + \omega^{-r} (1 + \omega)^6 + \omega^{-2r} (1 - 1)^6 + \omega^{-3r} (1 - \omega)^6 \right]
   \]

   Simplifying the terms:
   - \( (1 + 1)^6 = 2^6 = 64 \)
   - \( (1 - 1)^6 = 0 \)
   - \( (1 + \omega)^2 = 2i \Rightarrow (1 + \omega)^6 = (2i)^3 = 8i^3 = -8i \)
   - \( (1 - \omega)^2 = -2i \Rightarrow (1 - \omega)^6 = (-2i)^3 = -8i^3 = 8i \)

   Therefore:

   \[
   T_r = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + (-i)^r (-8i) + i^r (8i) \right]
   \]

   For each \( r \):

   - \( r = 0 \):
     \[
     T_0 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + (-i)^0 (-8i) + i^0 (8i) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + (-8i) + 8i \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 \right] = 16
     \]

   - \( r = 1 \):
     \[
     T_1 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + (-i)^1 (-8i) + i^1 (8i) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + 8 + (-8) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 - 16 \right] = 12
     \]

   - \( r = 2 \):
     \[
     T_2 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + (-i)^2 (-8i) + i^2 (8i) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + 8i + (-8i) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 \right] = 16
     \]

   - \( r = 3 \):
     \[
     T_3 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + (-i)^3 (-8i) + i^3 (8i) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + 8 + 8 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 64 + 16 \right] = 20
     \]

Each transaction is represented as a dictionary with the following keys:
- `user_id`: a unique identifier for the user.
- `product_id`: a unique identifier for the product.
- `quantity`: the number of units of the product purchased.
- `price`: the price per unit of the product.
- `timestamp`: the timestamp of the transaction.

Your task is to implement a function `calculate_total_revenue(transactions)` that computes the total revenue generated by each user. The function should return a dictionary where the keys are `user_id`s and the values are the total revenue generated by each user.

**Input:**
- A list of transactions, where each transaction is a dictionary with the keys `user_id`, `product_id`, `quantity`, `price`, and `timestamp`.

**Output:**
- A dictionary with `user_id`s as keys and the total revenue generated by each user as values.

**Sample Input:**
```python
transactions = [
    {"user_id": 1, "product_id": 101, "quantity": 2, "price": 29.99, "timestamp": "2021-01-15T14:23:01Z"},
    {"user_id": 1, "product_id": 102, "quantity": 1, "price": 15.50, "timestamp": "2021-01-15T15:45:23Z"},
    {"user_id": 2, "product_id": 101, "quantity": 3, "price": 29.99, "timestamp": "2021-01-15T17:00:00Z"}
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{1: 75.48, 2: 89.97}
```

```python
def calculate_total_revenue(transactions):
    """
    Calculate the total revenue generated by each user from a list of transactions.

    :param transactions: List of dictionaries, each representing a transaction with keys:
                         'user_id', 'product_id', 'quantity', 'price', and 'timestamp'.
    :return: Dictionary with user_id as keys and total revenue as values.
    """
    total_revenue = {}
    
    for transaction in transactions:
        user_id = transaction['user_id']
        quantity = transaction['quantity']
        price = transaction['price']
        
        current_revenue = quantity * price
        
        if user_id in total_revenue:
            total_revenue[user_id] += current_revenue
        else:
            total_revenue[user_id] = current_revenue
    
    return total_revenue

# Example usage
transactions = [
    {"user_id": 1, "product_id": 101, "quantity": 2, "price": 29.99, "timestamp": "2021-01-15T14:23:01Z"},
    {"user_id": 1, "product_id": 102, "quantity": 1, "price": 15.50, "timestamp": "2021-01-15T15:45:23Z"},
    {"user_id": 2, "product_id": 101, "quantity": 3, "price": 29.99, "timestamp": "2021-01-15T17:00:00Z"}
]

print(calculate_total_revenue(transactions))

4. **Compute Coefficients for Basses:**
   - For basses, the generating function is \( (1 + x)^8 \).
   - We need to compute \( B_r \) for \( r = 0, 1, 2, 3 \).

   \[
   B_r = \frac{1}{4} \left[ (1 + 1)^8 + \omega^{-r} (1 + \omega)^8 + \omega^{-2r} (1 - 1)^8 + \omega^{-3r} (1 - \omega)^8 \right]
   \]

   Simplifying the terms:
   - \( (1 + 1)^8 = 2^8 = 256 \)
   - \( (1 - 1)^8 = 0 \)
   - \( (1 + \omega)^2 = 2i \Rightarrow (1 + \omega)^8 = (2i)^4 = 16 \)
   - \( (1 - \omega)^2 = -2i \Rightarrow (1 - \omega)^8 = (-2i)^4 = 16 \)

   Therefore:

   \[
   B_r = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-i)^r \cdot 16 + i^r \cdot 16 \right]
   \]

   For each \( r \):

   - \( r = 0 \):
     \[
     B_0 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-i)^0 \cdot 16 + i^0 \cdot 16 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + 16 + 16 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 288 \right] = 72
     \]

   - \( r = 1 \):
     \[
     B_1 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-i)^1 \cdot 16 + i^1 \cdot 16 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-16) + 16 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 \right] = 64
     \]

   - \( r = 2 \):
     \[
     B_2 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-i)^2 \cdot 16 + i^2 \cdot 16 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-16) + (-16) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 224 \right] = 56
     \]

   - \( r = 3 \):
     \[
     B_3 = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + (-i)^3 \cdot 16 + i^3 \cdot 16 \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 + 16 + (-16) \right] = \frac{1}{4} \left[ 256 \right] = 64
     \]

5. **Sum the Products:**
   - The total number of valid groups is the sum of the products \( T_r \times B_r \) for \( r = 0, 1, 2, 3 \), minus 1 for the empty group.

   \[
   N = (T_0 \times B_0) + (T_1 \times B_1) + (T_2 \times B_2) + (T_3 \times B_3) - 1
   \]

   \[
   N = (16 \times 72) + (12 \times 64) + (16 \times 56) + (20 \times 64) - 1
   \]

   \[
   N = 1152 + 768 + 896 + 1280 - 1 = 4095
   \]

6. **Find the Remainder:**
   - The remainder when \( N \) is divided by 100 is:

   \[
   4095 \mod 100 = 95
   \]

Therefore, the remainder when \( N \) is divided by 100 is:

\boxed{95}

<#sep#># Limiting power output and storm protection

Energy2green Wind And Solar Power System

Get Instant Access

The power that can be taken from the wind varies with the wind speed. After reaching the nominal power, the power should remain constant for wind speeds greater than the nominal wind speed because the turbine and generator cannot handle more power. Therefore, a wind power plant must limit the power with one of the two following methods:

• stall control

Figure 5.14 Stall Effect at Higher Wind Speeds

Stall control takes advantage of the stall effect that occurs at high angles of attack (see Figure 5.14). This effect destroys the buoyancy force and thus limits the power that the wind transfers to the rotor blade. The rotor's rotational speed n and the circumferential speed u remain constant for stall-controlled wind generators. Stall control is achieved by increasing the angle of attack at higher wind speeds vW. The rotor blades do not pitch, i.e. the pitch angle remains constant; stall control can be realized by construction measures without advanced technical requirements. The disadvantage of stall control is the low possibility to influence operation because stall control is purely passive. The maximum power of a newly designed rotor blade is not easy to estimate because a mathematical description of the stall effect is rather difficult. After reaching the maximum power, the power output of stall-controlled wind turbines decreases again and does not remain on a constant level as shown in Figure 5.13.

Many manufacturers of wind turbines prefer pitch control, although the technical effort is much higher than for stall control. However, since pitch control is an active control, it can be adjusted to suit the conditions, in contrast to stall-controlled systems. Pitch control directly increases or lowers the pitch angle of the rotor and therefore the angle of attack. The rotor blade is turned into the wind at higher wind speeds (see Figure 5.15), lowering the angle of attack and actively decreasing the power input of the rotor blade. Pitch-controlled wind turbines are more difficult to manufacture because the rotor blades must twist inside the rotor hub. Small systems often use mechanically controlled pitch mechanisms using centrifugal forces. An electric motor moves the rotor blade to the desired position in large systems.

If the wind generator is stopped due to storm protection, the pitch control can pitch the rotor blade towards the feather position. This reduces the power input and avoids damage to the wind turbine. Stall-controlled systems often have additional aerodynamic brakes. For instance, the rotor tip can bend. During storms, the tip bends by 90° and slows the wind turbine.

Figure 5.15 Rotor Blade Positions for Different Wind Speeds for a Pitch-controlled System<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that supports basic banking operations. The class should allow deposits, withdrawals, and balance checks. Additionally, it should maintain a transaction history and ensure that the account balance never goes negative. 

**Operations:**
- `deposit(amount)`: Adds the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive.
- `withdraw(amount)`: Subtracts the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
- `check_balance()`: Returns the current balance of the account.
- `print_transaction_history()`: Prints all transactions (deposits and withdrawals) along with the final balance.

**Constraints:**
- The account balance must not be negative after any transaction.
- All monetary amounts should be handled as positive numbers.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
account = BankAccount(100)
account.deposit(50)
account.withdraw(30)
account.print_transaction_history()
```
**Expected Output:**
```
Transaction History:
Deposited: $50.00
Withdrew: $30.00
Current Balance: $120.00
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        self.balance = initial_balance
        self.transaction_history = []

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Adds the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            self.transaction_history.append(f'Deposited: ${amount:.2f}')
        else:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Subtracts the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is not positive or if there are insufficient funds.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                self.transaction_history.append(f'Withdrew: ${amount:.2f}')
            else:
                raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        else:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: A string representing the current balance.
        """
        return f'Current Balance: ${self.balance:.2f}'

    def print_transaction_history(self):
        """
        Prints all transactions (deposits and withdrawals) along with the final balance.
        """
        print("Transaction History:")
        for transaction in self.transaction_history:
            print(transaction)
        print(self.check_balance())

First, the problem involves absolute values and three variables. The constraint is the equation of a sphere with radius √2. Since we're dealing with maxima and minima on a sphere, maybe using Lagrange multipliers could be a way to go. But the absolute values complicate things because they can make the function non-differentiable at certain points. Alternatively, maybe I can consider symmetry or specific cases where variables are equal or have certain relationships to simplify the problem.

Let me start by considering symmetry. If x, y, z are all equal, then x = y = z. But substituting into the sphere equation, we get 3x² = 2 => x = ±√(2/3). Then |x−y| = 0, |y−z| = 0, |z−x| = 0, so P = (0 + 9)(0 + 9)(0 + 9) = 729. But is this the maximum? Let’s define f(s) = sqrt[(2 - s²)/2] + s. To find the maximum of f(s), take derivative:

f'(s) = (1/2)[(2 - s²)/2]^(-1/2) * (-2s)/2 + 1 = (-s)/(2*sqrt((2 - s²)/2)) + 1.

Simplify sqrt((2 - s²)/2) = sqrt(1 - s²/2). So,

f'(s) = (-s)/(2*sqrt(1 - s²/2)) + 1.

Set derivative equal to zero:

(-s)/(2*sqrt(1 - s²/2)) + 1 = 0

=> (s)/(2*sqrt(1 - s²/2)) = 1

Multiply both sides by 2*sqrt(1 - s²/2):

s = 2*sqrt(1 - s²/2)

Square both sides:

s² = 4*(1 - s²/2)

s² = 4 - 2s²

3s² = 4

s² = 4/3

s = 2/sqrt(3) ≈ 1.1547, but since s ≤ √2 ≈ 1.4142, this is acceptable.

Currently, moto allows subscribing a non-existing endpoint to a topic when the `application` protocol is used. It would be nice if moto could match the behavior on AWS. On AWS, an `InvalidParameterException` will be raised with an error response ` An error occurred (InvalidParameter) when calling the Subscribe operation: Invalid parameter: Endpoint Reason: Endpoint does not exist for endpoint {endpoint_arn}`

**Example code:** 
```python
client = boto3.client("sns")

platform_application_arn = client.create_platform_application(
    Name="test",
    Platform="GCM",
    Attributes={},
)["PlatformApplicationArn"]

endpoint_arn = client.create_platform_endpoint(
    PlatformApplicationArn=platform_application_arn,
    Token="test-token",
)["EndpointArn"]

topic_arn = client.create_topic(Name="test-topic")["TopicArn"]

client.subscribe(
    TopicArn=topic_arn,
    Endpoint=endpoint_arn,
    Protocol="application",
)  # Works as expected

client.delete_endpoint(EndpointArn=endpoint_arn)

client.subscribe(
    TopicArn=topic_arn,
    Endpoint=endpoint_arn,
    Protocol="application",
) # Should raise InvalidParameterException

```

**Expected result:** The last call to `subscribe` should raise `InvalidParameterException`

**Moto version:** 5.0.3

<#sep#># FORMULE TRIGONOMETRICE PDF

Main article: History of trigonometry Hipparchus , credited with compiling the first trigonometric table , has been described as "the father of trigonometry". The ancient Nubians used a similar method. In BC, Hipparchus from Nicaea , Asia Minor gave the first tables of chords, analogous to modern tables of sine values , and used them to solve problems in trigonometry and spherical trigonometry. The modern sine convention is first attested in the Surya Siddhanta , and its properties were further documented by the 5th century AD Indian mathematician and astronomer Aryabhata. By the 10th century, Islamic mathematicians were using all six trigonometric functions, had tabulated their values, and were applying them to problems in spherical geometry.

 Author: Fenos Akile Country: Andorra Language: English (Spanish) Genre: Travel Published (Last): 15 January 2018 Pages: 332 PDF File Size: 11.51 Mb ePub File Size: 16.16 Mb ISBN: 818-6-80906-407-9 Downloads: 46952 Price: Free* [*Free Regsitration Required] Uploader: Kabei

Bottom: Graph of sine function versus angle. Angles from the top panel are identified. Radians versus degrees[ edit ] In geometric applications, the argument of a trigonometric function is generally the measure of an angle. For this purpose, any angular unit is convenient, and angles are most commonly measured in degrees. When using trigonometric function in calculus , their argument is generally not an angle, but rather a real number. In this case, it is more suitable to express the argument of the trigonometric as the length of the arc of the unit circle delimited by an angle with the center of the circle as vertex.

Therefore, one uses the radian as angular unit: a radian is the angle that delimits an arc of length 1 on the unit circle. A great advantage of radians is that many formulas are much simpler when using them, typically all formulas relative to derivatives and integrals. This is thus a general convention that, when the angular unit is not explicitly specified, the arguments of trigonometric functions are always expressed in radians. Signs of trigonometric functions in each quadrant.

The mnemonic "all science teachers are crazy" lists the functions which are positive from quadrants I to IV. The six trigonometric functions can be defined as coordinate values of points on the Euclidean plane that are related to the unit circle , which is the circle of radius one centered at the origin O of this coordinate system.

.<#sep#>You are tasked with developing a system to compute the total revenue generated from online course sales for a specific month. The system will receive a list of sales records, where each record includes the course ID, the sale date, the course price, and any applicable discount. Your function should return the total revenue for the specified month. Assume the input list is non-empty and all dates are formatted as `YYYY-MM-DD`.

**Input:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains:
  - `course_id`: A string representing the course identifier.
  - `sale_date`: A string representing the date of the sale in the format `YYYY-MM-DD`.
  - `price`: A float representing the original price of the course.
  - `discount`: A float representing the discount percentage (e.g., 0.20 for a 20% discount).
- A string `target_month` representing the month for which to calculate the revenue in the format `YYYY-MM`.

**Output:**
- A float representing the total revenue generated in the specified month.

**Sample Input:**
```python
sales = [
    ('CS101', '2023-10-01', 79.99, 0.1),
    ('CS201', '2023-10-05', 129.99, 0.0),
    ('CS101', '2023-09-20', 79.99, 0.05),
    ('CS301', '2023-10-15', 99.99, 0.0),
    ('CS102', '2023-10-25', 89.99, 0.15),
]
target_month = '2023-10'
```

**Sample Output:**
```
404.91
```

```python
from datetime import datetime

def calculate_monthly_revenue(sales_records, target_month):
    """
    Calculate the total revenue generated from course sales for a given month.

    :param sales_records: List of sales records, where each record is a tuple (course_id, date, price, discount).
                          `date` is a string in the format 'YYYY-MM-DD'.
                          `price` is a float representing the original price of the course.
                          `discount` is a float representing the discount percentage (e.g., 0.20 for 20% discount).
    :param target_month: String representing the target month in the format 'YYYY-MM'.
    :return: Total revenue generated in the specified month.
    """
    total_revenue = 0.0

    for course_id, sale_date, price, discount in sales_records:
        sale_datetime = datetime.strptime(sale_date, '%Y-%m-%d')
        sale_month = sale_datetime.strftime('%Y-%m')
        
        if sale_month == target_month:
            final_price = price * (1 - discount)
            total_revenue += final_price

    return total_revenue

# Example usage:
sales = [
    ('CS101', '2023-10-01', 79.99, 0.1),
    ('CS201', '2023-10-05', 129.99, 0.0),
    ('CS101', '2023-09-20', 79.99, 0.05),
    ('CS301', '2023-10-15', 99.99, 0.0),
    ('CS102', '2023-10-25', 89.99, 0.15),
]

target_month = '2023-10'
print(calculate_monthly_revenue(sales, target_month))  # Output should be 404.91

<#sep#>S3 CopyObject in place with Checksum raises an exception
Since adding the checksum to CopyObject, moto will raise an exception if you try to copy an object in place while requesting its checksum, because it tries to use the source key `.value`, but that key has been overwritten by the new one copied one in place.

Here's the traceback: 
```python

E             File "/opt/code/localstack/.venv/lib/python3.10/site-packages/moto/s3/responses.py", line 1266, in _key_response

E             File "/usr/local/lib/python3.10/tempfile.py", line 779, in seek
E               return self._file.seek(*args)
E           ValueError: I/O operation on closed file.
```

I'll try to open a PR shortly. 

To reproduce:

```python
s3_client.put_object(
    Bucket=bucket_name,
    Key=object_key,
    Body='{"key": "value"}',
)

# copy the object in place with some metadata and replacing it, but with a checksum
s3_client.copy_object(
    Bucket=bucket_name,
    CopySource=f"{bucket_name}/{object_key}",
    Key=object_key,
    ChecksumAlgorithm="SHA256",
    MetadataDirective="REPLACE",
)
```

<#sep#># How do you verify sinthetacosthetatantheta+cos^2theta=1?

Nov 12, 2016

see below

#### Explanation:

$\sin \theta \cos \theta \tan \theta + {\cos}^{2} \theta = 1$

Left Side:$= \sin \theta \cos \theta \tan \theta + {\cos}^{2} \theta$

$= \sin \theta \cos \theta \sin \frac{\theta}{\cos} \theta + {\cos}^{2} \theta$

$= \sin \theta \cancel{\cos} \theta \sin \frac{\theta}{\cancel{\cos}} \theta + {\cos}^{2} \theta$

$= {\sin}^{2} \theta + {\cos}^{2} \theta$

$= 1$

$\therefore =$Right Side<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `DatabaseConnection` that manages SQLite database connections. The class should support opening and closing connections, executing SQL queries, and handling exceptions. Additionally, it should implement the context manager protocol to allow usage with a `with` statement. The class should provide the following methods:

- `__init__(self, db_name)`: Initializes the database connection with the provided database name.
- `open(self)`: Opens the database connection.
- `close(self)`: Closes the database connection.
- `execute(self, query, params=None)`: Executes a given SQL query with optional parameters and returns the cursor object. If an error occurs, it should print an error message and return `None`.
- `__enter__(self)`: Returns the `DatabaseConnection` object when used with a `with` statement.
- `__exit__(self, exc_type, exc_val, exc_tb)`: Closes the database connection when the block of code is exited and returns `False` to propagate any exceptions.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: 
  ```python
  with DatabaseConnection('example.db') as db:
      query = "CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS test (id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY, data TEXT)"
      db.execute(query)
      query = "INSERT INTO test (data) VALUES (?)"
      db.execute(query, ("example data",))
      query = "SELECT * FROM test"
      results = db.execute(query)
      for row in results:
          print(row)
  ```
- Output:
  ```
  Opened database connection to example.db
  Closed database connection to example.db
  (1, 'example data')
  ```

```python
import sqlite3

class DatabaseConnection:
    def __init__(self, db_name):
        """
        Initializes the database connection with the provided database name.
        
        :param db_name: Name of the SQLite database file.
        """
        self.db_name = db_name
        self.connection = None

    def open(self):
        """
        Opens the database connection.
        """
        try:
            self.connection = sqlite3.connect(self.db_name)
            print(f"Opened database connection to {self.db_name}")
        except sqlite3.Error as e:
            print(f"Failed to open database connection: {e}")

    def close(self):
        """
        Closes the database connection.
        """
        if self.connection:
            self.connection.close()
            print(f"Closed database connection to {self.db_name}")
            self.connection = None

    def execute(self, query, params=None):
        """
        Executes a given SQL query with optional parameters.
        
        :param query: SQL query to be executed.
        :param params: Optional parameters for the SQL query.
        :return: Cursor object if successful, None otherwise.
        """
        try:
            cursor = self.connection.cursor()
            if params:
                cursor.execute(query, params)
            else:
                cursor.execute(query)
            self.connection.commit()
            return cursor
        except sqlite3.Error as e:
            print(f"Failed to execute query: {e}")
            return None

    def __enter__(self):
        """
        Returns the DatabaseConnection object when used with a `with` statement.
        
        :return: DatabaseConnection object.
        """
        self.open()
        return self

    def __exit__(self, exc_type, exc_val, exc_tb):
        """
        Closes the database connection when the block of code is exited.
        
        :param exc_type: Exception type.
        :param exc_val: Exception value.
        :param exc_tb: Exception traceback.
        :return: False to propagate any exceptions.
        """
        self.close()
        return False  # Propagate exceptions if they occur

 Continuing this way, for each step:

 the DP step for the current clause pulls in the previous state’s possibilities and, for each possibility, calculates how the current clause contributes to the XOR, and the next outgoing var (its right connector).

 This way, after processing all clauses in the chain, the final DP will give for each possible XOR value (0 and/or1) and the final outgoing variable's value (but since we're at the end, that final variable's value doesn’t matters: as long as the XOR is tracked).

 The final count is summed over all entries in the DP that have the desired XOR (such as the total's S0 and S1 for this chain-component) we can get S0 and S1 values.

 So the S0 of the component is the sum of all paths in the DP that end with total XOR 0.

 Similarly S1 is paths ending with XOR 1.

 The final variables not involved in connectors (exclusives) are already accounted for in the paths.

 Now, the problem also has the possibility of cycles. When the component is a cycle, the first and last clauses are connected. This adds a complexity, since the start and end connectors are connected.

 In this case, the above linear approach would miss the cyclical constraint.

 Thus, for cycles:

 We can break the cycle at some edge (turning it into a chain by breaking one link), then accounting for the additional constraint that the first and last variables must satisfy the XOR across all clauses, plus the cycle's link.

 This requires tracking the variable's value at both ends and ensuring consistency.

 Alternatively, perform the DP for a chain and multiply by constraints based on the closed condition.

 This approach might involve doubling the DP or other clever steps.

But implementing this for both cycles and chains requires that we determine the structure of each component, then apply the appropriate DP.

So the steps would be:

 For each component:

   Determine whether it forms a chain or cycle.

   If it’s a chain:

      Proceed with the linear DP to compute S0 and S1.

   If it’s a cycle:

      Break the cycle into a chain (remove one connecting variable) and run the DP twice: once assuming the two ends are 0 and once as 1, then combine applicably.

      Or better approach:

      To break a cycle into a chain and have the additional condition that the first and last variable must agree.

      This introduces the need to track the first variable’s value and ensure it matches the last.

      So, the cycle’s DP must account for this.

      Hence， the DP would need to track both the ending var's value and the starting var's value.

      This could increase the complexity.

 However, even with cycles, perhaps the DP can be extended by ensuring a full-wrap around.

This seems feasible.

 The challenges are:

 First， to represent the component's structure.

Second， to implement the DP efficiently, given that the component's size can be up to 1e5 variables, but perhaps in practice manageable with clever code.

But given that the overall variables are limited (since each variable can only be in 2 clauses up to), but given n and m are up to one hundred thousand, this is quite big.

Wait the constraints of the problem are n and m up to 1e5. So need to have an O(n) per component?

This is important, so the system must be fast enough.

First, let’s consider the steps to model:

First, for each component’s representation as a graph of clauses and variables， we need to decide whether it's a chain or a cycle, and choose the appropriate DP.

Thus, the algorithm steps would be:

1. Build the graph for each clause.

Each node represents a clause. An edge between two clauses represents a variable shared between them.

The components (connected components) can be found via BFS or DFS.

Once we've found all connected components， for each component， we can determine if it's a chain or cycle.

Wait the edges are formed by variables present in two clauses. So the graph is made up of undirected edges between clauses that share a variable.

The connected component can thus be a single node, a chain, or a cycle.

For determining whether the component is cyclic:

Compute the number of edges: 

For a connected component with e edges and v nodes:

- For a tree: edges = vertices -1 → not cyclical.

- If edges == vertices → it's a single cycle (e.g., in a component of a single cycle, each contributes 1 edge between consecutive nodes, and total edges: v= number of clauses, e = v edges. Since it's a cyjective, edges= vertices.

 Thus， if the component's graph has edges (edges) equal to vertices (v)， and is connected, → it's a cycle.

 Else, it's a chain (tree).

Thus, the determination is:

For connected components:

if edges == vertices： it's a cycle.

else if edges == vertices-1 →chain.

 else: ?

 Oh wait, but for example a chain can have edges = vertices-1.

 A tree is acyclic. So:

 connected components with edges < vertices can still be chains if they form a tree. But in our case, the clauses graph for each component can only be edges which form simple paths or cycles.

 Because each variable can only be in two clauses → that edge is only between two clauses.

 Hence, the components are either cycles (each has exactly edges = vertices) or paths (edges = vertices-1).

Therefore, to determine the structure:

for each connected component (represented as edges and vertices count):

  if edges == vertices: it's a cycle.

  else (since edges must be vertices-1 for connected components → assuming it's connected):

   it's a chain.

 -> Thus that's how we can categorize the components.

 Once the type is known，we can proceed with DP for the component.

Now, to proceed with the coding steps:

First, the main challenges：

1. Decomposing the clauses into their connected components and detecting whether each is a cycle or chain.

2. For each component, run DP to calculate S0 and S1.

 Now, let's think about the DP implementation details.

Let me focus first on DP for a chain component.

Let's take an example, and see if I can outline the DP:

Chain: clauses C1, C2, ..., Ck connected in a line.

Each pair has connecting variables.

We will model the clauses in order.

For each step i (processing the first i clauses up to C_i), the DP state must track:

- The current XOR value accumulated up to the first i clauses.

- The value of the variable outgoing from C_i (which will be the connector variable to C_{i+1}).

 Thus, the state can be represented as a pair (parity, var_val).

 The state is represented as (current_xor, outgoing_var_val). 

 The DP table will be a dictionary（or array） for each possible parity (0 or1), and var_val (0 or1), keeping track of the number of ways to reach there.

初始化：

Initially (before any clauses), the starting point is before C1.

At step 0 (no clauses):

 parity is 0 (since XOR of zero terms is 0).

 outgoing_var_val is some non-existent, but since we are starting, before C1, the first clause's incoming connector is none. Instead, when processing C1, its own outgoing variable must be determined.

 Thus, starting the recursive steps.

 Let's start with processing C1.

 The DP starts at step 0 with state (0, nothing) but when considering processing C1， the outgoing variable is chosen,

 the initial state is the original 0.

 For C1：

 it has its own literals, including its "left" variable (<#sep#>Creating mock AutoScalingGroups doesn't allow string versions for Launch templates

<#sep#># Help me with this graph problem.

Someone mentioned in an answer that the following problem can be solved using only DFS/BFS: http://www.spoj.com/problems/PRATA/

I am not getting any idea on how to solve this problem without using Dijkstra’s algo/ MST. Binary Search is another approach. Can anyone tell how to solve this using only BFS/DFS?

1 Like

Are you sure there is a way to use MST in this problem?

There are only 8 ranks and chefs with the same rank are indistinguishable. So instead of L chefs, we can just say we have count[i] chefs of rank i.

For each rank i, we will get count[i] pratas at times i, 3i, 6i, etc.

So, we can just simulate the cooking with a Breadth First Search until we cook P pratas.

``````First initialize next_cooking_time[rank i] = i.
Number of pratas cooked = 0
while we are not done
Find minimum next_cooking_time[i for in 1..8]
number_cooked += count[best_rank]
if number_cooked >= P
Update next_cooking_time[best_rank]
``````

Since there is a constant number (8) of ranks, this algorithm works in O§.

PS: Note that with such a small number of ranks, using something like a priority queue is slower due to the constant factors.

Hello ketanhwr,

I read the problem and find that this problem can be solved using binary search …Submit it and Got AC. I will pasting link to my accepted solution at the end of this post .

How does this idea comes to my mind…

Think this way … Let us assume that T is the minimum time required to make P parathas . So , We can also make P parathas in T+1 minutes , T+2 minutes and so on but it is gurantee that we cannot make P parathas in T-1 minutes because otherwise T is not the minimum time in which we can make P parathas…

So what does this signifies … We can binary search over the time … and can check whether we can make P parathas in X minutes and cannot make P parathas in T-1 minutes then break this is the minimum time …else if we cannot make P parathas in given time then move to right side and else move to the left side …

Here is my code which does the following …

Look at this code fragment. high = 100000000 is set as an upper bound only …
Assuming that P >= 1.
Consider for now that you have a function check which only tells you whether you can make P parathas in given time X .

Hope you got the idea of binary search.

```            int low,high,mid ;
low = 1;
high = 100000000 ;
while(low<=high){

mid = (low+high)/2 ;
if(check(mid) && (mid == 1 || !check(mid-1)))
break ;
else if(check(mid))
high = mid-1;
else
low = mid+1;
}
ans = mid ;
```

Let us discuss check function for now …

Here is my check function for the above code …

I have done thing but just check each cook can produce how many parathas in the given time and calculate the total count and compare it with P.

May be it seems complex as i used upper_bound function but it is very simple function .

```bool check(int x){

int total = 0;
for(int i=1;i<=N;i++){
total += (upper_bound(AP[A[i]]+1,AP[A[i]]+1+1000,x)-(AP[A[i]]+1));
}
return (total>=P) ;
}

```

Considering you are already familiar with this upper_bound function if you are not it is a variation of a binary search which always given iterator to the values which is strictly greater than the search value if present …

AP is nothing but a 2-D array which stores AP[i][j] stores how much does a cook having rank i takes to prepare j parathas only …

Hope you got the idea about the solution of this problem using binary search …

Here is my code link to the above problem code

Thanx for this great solution i have not thought that way …<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple bank account system in Python. Your program should support the following operations:

1. Create an account with an initial balance.
2. Deposit a specified amount into the account.
3. Withdraw a specified amount from the account, ensuring that the balance does not go below zero.
4. Display the current balance of the account.
5. Display the account information, including the account holder's name and account number.

**Input:**
- The first line contains the account holder's name.
- The second line contains the account number.
- The third line contains the initial balance.
- The fourth line contains the number of operations to perform.
- The following lines contain the operations, each on a new line. Each operation is either:
  - `deposit <amount>`: Deposit the specified amount into the account.
  - `withdraw <amount>`: Withdraw the specified amount from the account.
  - `balance`: Display the current balance.
  - `info`: Display the account information.

**Output:**
- For each `deposit` and `withdraw` operation, print the updated balance or an error message.
- For each `balance` operation, print the current balance.
- For each `info` operation, print the account holder's name, account number, and balance.

**Sample Input:**
```
John Smith
123456789
1000.00
5
deposit 500
withdraw 200
withdraw 1500
balance
info
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Deposited $500.00. New balance is $1500.00.
Withdrew $200.00. New balance is $1300.00.
Insufficient funds.
Current balance is $1300.00.
Account Holder: John Smith
Account Number: 123456789
Balance: $1300.00
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_holder_name, account_number, initial_balance=0.0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with the given account holder's name, account number, and initial balance.
        """
        self.account_holder_name = account_holder_name
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited ${amount:.2f}. New balance is ${self.balance:.2f}.")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account, ensuring the balance does not go below zero.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if amount <= self.balance:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew ${amount:.2f}. New balance is ${self.balance:.2f}.")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def display_balance(self):
        """
        Display the current balance of the account.
        """
        print(f"Current balance is ${self.balance:.2f}.")

    def display_account_info(self):
        """
        Display the account information, including the account holder's name, account number, and balance.
        """
        print(f"Account Holder: {self.account_holder_name}")
        print(f"Account Number: {self.account_number}")
        print(f"Balance: ${self.balance:.2f}")

# Example usage:
account = BankAccount("John Smith", "123456789", 1000.00)
account.display_account_info()
account.deposit(500)
account.withdraw(200)
account.withdraw(1500)
account.display_balance()

## Mental Mondays #3

This Mental and Oral activity revolves around number bonds (or complements) to 100.

Children work in pairs, using a mini-whiteboard. Ask them to draw a three by three grid and to quickly fill the grid with different multiples of 5. When they have completed this the teacher starts to call out random multiples of 5, children can circle a number on their grid if it is the other half of the pair totalling exactly 100 (the complement to 100). Read more »<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class for a library management system. The class should have the following attributes:
- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN number of the book in the format '978-3-16-148410-0'.
- `available`: A boolean indicating whether the book is available for checkout.

Implement the following methods:
- `checkout()`: Checks out the book if it is available. If the book is not available, it should print a message indicating that the book is not available for checkout.
- `return_book()`: Returns the book to make it available again. If the book was not checked out, it should print a message indicating that the book was not checked out.
- `display_details()`: Displays the details of the book, including its title, author, ISBN, and availability status.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Input:
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "978-3-16-148410-0")
book.display_details()
book.checkout()
book.display_details()
book.return_book()
book.display_details()

Output:
Title: 1984
Author: George Orwell
ISBN: 978-3-16-148410-0
Status: Available
'1984' has been checked out.
Title: 1984
Author: George Orwell
ISBN: 978-3-16-148410-0
Status: Not Available
'1984' has been returned.
Title: 1984
Author: George Orwell
ISBN: 978-3-16-148410-0
Status: Available
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, available=True):
        """
        Initialize a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book (str)
        :param author: The author of the book (str)
        :param isbn: The ISBN number of the book (str)
        :param available: Boolean indicating if the book is available (bool)
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.available = available

    def checkout(self):
        """
        Check out the book if it is available.
        """
        if self.available:
            self.available = False
            print(f"'{self.title}' has been checked out.")
        else:
            print(f"'{self.title}' is not available for checkout.")

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Return the book to make it available.
        """
        if not self.available:
            self.available = True
            print(f"'{self.title}' has been returned.")
        else:
            print(f"'{self.title}' was not checked out.")

    def display_details(self):
        """
        Display the details of the book.
        """
        availability = "Available" if self.available else "Not Available"
        print(f"Title: {self.title}")
        print(f"Author: {self.author}")
        print(f"ISBN: {self.isbn}")
        print(f"Status: {availability}")
```<#sep#> possible signs. Let me take another tactic. Let's use Lagrange multipliers. Let's denote f(x,y,z) = (|x - y| +9)(|y - z| +9)(|z -x| +9) and we need to maximize this subject to g(x,y,z)=x² + y² + z² -2=0.

The problem is that the absolute values make the function non-differentiable at certain points. However, perhaps the maximum occurs at a point where x, y, z are such that x ≠ y, y ≠ z, z ≠ x, so the absolute values can be treated as differentiable functions by considering the appropriate signs. Alternatively, if we assume an order, like x ≥ y ≥ z, then |x - y|=x - y, |y - z|=y - z, |z -x|=x - z. Then f becomes (x - y +9)(y - z +9)(x - z +9). Then we can set up the Lagrangian: L = (x - y +9)(y - z +9)(x - z +9) - λ(x² + y² + z² -2).

Then take partial derivatives with respect to x, y, z, set to zero.

But this might be very complicated. Let's see.

First, let's compute the partial derivatives.

Let’s denote A = x - y +9, B = y - z +9, C = x - z +9. Note that C = A + B -9.

Then L = A*B*C - λ(x² + y² + z² -2).

Compute ∂L/∂x = B*C + A*C + A*B*(dC/dx) - 2λx. Wait, no. Wait, actually, when differentiating A*B*C, where A =x - y +9, B = y - z +9, C =x - z +9.

So, dA/dx =1, dB/dx=0, dC/dx=1.

Similarly, d/dx (A*B*C) = B*C*dA/dx + A*C*dB/dx + A*B*dC/dx = B*C*1 +0 +A*B*1 = B*C +A*B.

Similarly, ∂L/∂x = B*C + A*B - 2λx =0

Similarly, ∂L/∂y:

dA/dy= -1, dB/dy=1, dC/dy=0.

Thus, d/dy (A*B*C) = -B*C + A*C*1 +0 = -B*C +A*C.

But wait:

No, wait, product rule:

d/dy (A*B*C) = (dA/dy)*B*C + A*(dB/dy)*C + A*B*(dC/dy)

= (-1)*B*C + A*1*C + A*B*0 = -B*C +A*C.

Thus, ∂L/∂y = -B*C +A*C -2λy=0

Similarly, ∂L/∂z:

dA/dz=0, dB/dz= -1, dC/dz= -1

Thus, d/dz (A*B*C) =0*B*C +A*(-1)*C + A*B*(-1) = -A*C -A*B

Thus, ∂L/∂z = -A*C -A*B -2λz=0

So, the three equations are:

1. B*C +A*B -2λx =0

2. -B*C +A*C -2λy =0

3. -A*C -A*B -2λz =0

And the constraint x² + y² + z²=2.

This system seems complicated, but maybe assuming some symmetry.

Suppose x = -z. Then let's see. Let’s assume z = -x. Then the constraint becomes x² + y² +x²=2 =>2x² + y²=2. Also, since z = -x, compute the differences:

A = x - y +9

B = y - z +9 = y +x +9

C = x - z +9 =x +x +9=2x +9

So P = A*B*C = (x - y +9)(x + y +9)(2x +9)

Let’s see if this assumption leads to a solution. Also, check if the partial derivatives can be satisfied.

But even with this assumption, the equations are still complex. Let’s substitute z = -x into the Lagrangian equations.

From 3. -A*C -A*B -2λz =0. Since z = -x,

Equation 3 becomes: -A*C -A*B +2λx =0.

Compare with equation 1: B*C +A*B -2λx =0.

If we add equations 1 and 3:

B*C +A*B -2λx + (-A*C -A*B +2λx)=0 => B*C -A*C =0 =>C*(B -A)=0.

So either C=0 or B=A.

But C=2x +9, which is zero only if x= -9/2. But x must satisfy 2x² + y²=2. If x= -9/2, then x²=81/4, which is way larger than 2. So C≠0. Therefore, B=A.

So B =A => y +x +9 =x - y +9 => y +x =x - y =>2y=0 => y=0.

So if we assume z = -x and y=0, then check if this satisfies the equations.

If y=0, z=-x, then constraint is 2x²=2 =>x²=1 =>x=1 or x=-1.

Compute P: x - y +9 =x -0 +9= x +9

B= y +x +9=0 +x +9=x +9

C=2x +9

So P=(x +9)^2*(2x +9)

If x=1: (1 +9)^2*(2 +9)=100*11=1100

If x=-1: (-1 +9)^2*(-2 +9)=64*7=448

So maximum at x=1, giving P=1100.

This matches our earlier case. Let’s check if the partial derivatives are satisfied here.

At x=1, y=0, z=-1:

A =1 -0 +9=10

B=0 - (-1) +9=10

C=1 - (-1) +9=11

Wait, no, wait in our substitution, when z=-x, and x=1, then z=-1. Then:

A=x - y +9=1 -0 +9=10

B=y - z +9=0 - (-1)+9=10

C=x - z +9=1 - (-1) +9=11

Thus, P=10*10*11=1100.

Now, compute the Lagrangian equations:

Equation 1: B*C +A*B -2λx =0.

Substitute A=10, B=10, C=11, x=1:

10*11 +10*10 -2λ*1 =110 +100 -2λ=210 -2λ=0 => λ=105.

Equation 2: -B*C +A*C -2λy =0.

Substitute B=10, C=11, A=10, y=0:

-10*11 +10*11 -2λ*0= -110 +110 -0=0. So equation 2 is satisfied.

Equation 3: -A*C -A*B +2λx=0.

Substitute A=10, C=11, B=10, x=1, λ=105:

-10*11 -10*10 +2*105*1= -110 -100 +210=0. Satisfied.

So the system is satisfied. Therefore, the point (1,0,-1) is a critical point. Similarly, permutations of variables would also be critical points, leading to the same P value. But we need to check if this is a maximum.

Given that we found another case where P≈1036.6 which is less than 1100, and the case with two variables 1 and -1, third zero gives 1100. But in this Lagrangian approach, we found a critical point at (1,0,-1) with P=1100, and also confirmed that the equations hold. So perhaps this is the maximum. But could there be other critical points with higher P?

MemberwiseClone (inherited from Object) Creates a shallow copy of the current Object.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a file change logging system. Your program should monitor file changes and log each event to a CSV file. Each log entry must include the timestamp, file path, and the type of change (e.g., created, modified, deleted). The CSV file should have a header row with the columns "timestamp", "file_path", and "change_type". The timestamp should be in ISO 8601 format (e.g., "2023-10-10T12:34:56").

Implement a Python class `FileChangeLogger` with the following specifications:
- The constructor `__init__` should take a single argument `log_file_path`, which is the path to the CSV file where logs will be stored.
- The method `log_change` should take two arguments: `file_path` (the path to the file that changed) and `change_type` (the type of change, e.g., 'created', 'modified', 'deleted'). This method should log the event with the current timestamp.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Given the following sequence of operations:
  ```python
  logger = FileChangeLogger('file_changes.csv')
  logger.log_change('/path/to/file.txt', 'created')
  logger.log_change('/path/to/file.txt', 'modified')
  ```
- The content of `file_changes.csv` should be:
  ```
  timestamp,file_path,change_type
  2023-10-10T12:34:56,/path/to/file.txt,created
  2023-10-10T12:35:00,/path/to/file.txt,modified
  ```
  (Note: The timestamps will vary based on the actual time of execution.)

```python
import csv
from datetime import datetime
import os

class FileChangeLogger:
    def __init__(self, log_file_path):
        """
        Initializes the FileChangeLogger with the path to the log file.
        
        :param log_file_path: str, the path to the CSV file where logs will be stored.
        """
        self.log_file_path = log_file_path
        self._ensure_csv_header()

    def _ensure_csv_header(self):
        """
        Ensures that the CSV file has the correct header row.
        """
        if not os.path.exists(self.log_file_path) or os.path.getsize(self.log_file_path) == 0:
            with open(self.log_file_path, mode='w', newline='') as file:
                writer = csv.writer(file)
                writer.writerow(["timestamp", "file_path", "change_type"])

    def log_change(self, file_path, change_type):
        """
        Logs a file change event with the current timestamp.
        
        :param file_path: str, the path to the file that changed.
        :param change_type: str, the type of change (e.g., 'created', 'modified', 'deleted').
        """
        timestamp = datetime.now().isoformat()
        with open(self.log_file_path, mode='a', newline='') as file:
            writer = csv.writer(file)
            writer.writerow([timestamp, file_path, change_type])
