Given that we found another case where P≈1036.6 which is less than 1100, and the case with two variables 1 and -1, third zero gives 1100. But in this Lagrangian approach, we found a critical point at (1,0,-1) with P=1100, and also confirmed that the equations hold. So perhaps this is the maximum. But could there be other critical points with higher P?

Alternatively, suppose we consider variables where two variables are equal, but we already saw that this gives a lower P. Or variables arranged with different differences. But given the Lagrangian approach yields 1100, and the other trials didn't surpass that, maybe 1100 is the maximum.

However, to be thorough, perhaps we can check another symmetric case. Suppose x, y, z are all different and non-zero, and we use the AM-GM inequality. But the problem is that P is a product of terms involving absolute differences, which complicates things.

Alternatively, note that each term in P is of the form |difference| +9. To maximize the product, since the terms are positive, it's better to have each term as large as possible. But the differences are constrained by the sphere equation.

Given that the variables are scalars, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, |x - y| ≤ √(2(x² + y²)). But since x² + y² + z²=2, x² + y²=2 - z². So |x - y| ≤√(2(2 - z²)). To maximize |x - y|, we need to maximize √(2(2 - z²)), which occurs when z=0, leading to |x - y| ≤√(4)=2. Which aligns with the previous case where |x - y|=2 when z=0. So indeed, the maximum possible |x - y| is 2, achieved when x=1, y=-1, z=0. Similarly for other pairs.

Therefore, in this case, one of the terms in P is maximized as 2 +9=11, and the other two differences are 1 each, leading to 10 each. Hence, P=11*10*10=1100.

Since all other differences cannot exceed 2, the maximum product P is at most (2 +9)(something +9)(something +9). However, to check if another configuration where two differences are larger than 1 but less than 2, and the third is also slightly larger, gives a higher product. Let's suppose we have |x−y|=1.5, |y−z|=1.5, |z−x|=3. But with x, y, z on the sphere x² + y² + z²=2, the differences are limited.

Wait, but if |x - z|=3, then since x and z are real numbers, the maximum |x - z| is limited by the sphere. Let's see. For two variables x and z, the maximum |x - z| occurs when one is sqrt(2) and the other is -sqrt(2), giving |x - z|=2sqrt(2)≈2.828. But in that case, the third variable y must be zero, leading to P=(|sqrt(2) - (-sqrt(2))| +9)(| -sqrt(2) -0| +9)(|0 - sqrt(2)| +9)= (2sqrt(2) +9)(sqrt(2)+9)(sqrt(2)+9). Calculating:

First term:2sqrt(2) +9≈2*1.414+9≈11.828

Second and third terms:1.414+9≈10.414

So P≈11.828*10.414*10.414≈11.828*108.39≈1283. This would be higher than 1100. Wait, but wait, in this case, x and z are sqrt(2) and -sqrt(2), but then x² + z²=2 +2=4, which exceeds the constraint x² + y² + z²=2. Therefore, this is impossible. So the maximum possible |x - z| is when y=0, and x² + z²=2. Then the maximum |x - z| is achieved when x and z are opposite, i.e., x=sqrt(1), z=-sqrt(1), but then x² + z²=2, so x=1, z=-1, y=0, which gives |x - z|=2, which is the same as before.

So the previous case where one difference is 2 and the other two are 1 each is indeed the maximum possible. Any attempt to have a larger difference would violate the sphere constraint.

Therefore, after considering various cases and verifying with Lagrange multipliers, the maximum value of P is 1100.


**Final Answer**
\boxed{1100}
</think>To find the maximum value of \( P = (|x-y| + 9)(|y-z| + 9)(|z-x| + 9) \) given the constraint \( x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 2 \), we will explore specific configurations of \(x\), \(y\), and \(z\) that maximize the differences \(|x-y|\), \(|y-z|\), and \(|z-x|\).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Consider the Constraint**:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 2
   \]

2. **Evaluate Specific Configurations**:
   - **Case 1: All variables equal**:
     \[
     x = y = z \implies 3x^2 = 2 \implies x = \pm \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}
     \]
     Then, \(|x - y| = 0\), \(|y - z| = 0\), \(|z - x| = 0\), leading to:
     \[
     P = (0 + 9)(0 + 9)(0 + 9) = 729
     \]
     This is not the maximum.

   - **Case 2: Two variables equal, one different**:
     Suppose \( x = y \). Then:
     \[
     2x^2 + z^2 = 2 \implies x^2 = \frac{2 - z^2}{2}
     \]
     To maximize \(|x - z|\), set \( x = \sqrt{\frac{2 - z^2}{2}} \). The differences are:
     \[
     |x - y| = 0, \quad |y - z| = |x - z|, \quad |z - x| = |x - z|
     \]
     Thus:
     \[
     P = 9 \times (|x - z| + 9)^2
     \]
     Maximizing \(|x - z|\) under the constraint \(2x^2 + z^2 = 2\) yields \(|x - z| \approx \sqrt{3}\), leading to:
     \[
     P \approx 9 \times (\sqrt{3} + 9)^2 \approx 1036.6
     \]
     This is higher but not the maximum.

   - **Case 3: Two variables opposites, third zero**:
     Suppose \( x = 1 \), \( y = -1 \), \( z = 0 \). Then:
     \[
     x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
     \]
     The differences are:
     \[
     |x - y| = 2, \quad |y - z| = 1, \quad |z - x| = 1
     \]
     Thus:
     \[
     P = (2 + 9)(1 + 9)(1 + 9) = 11 \times 10 \times 10 = 1100
     \]

3. **Verification**:
   - Using Lagrange multipliers, we confirm the critical point at \(x = 1\), \(y = 0\), \(z = -1\). The Lagrangian is:
     \[
     L = (|x - y| + 9)(|y - z| + 9)(|z - x| + 9) - \lambda (x^2 + y^2 + z^2 - 2)
     \]
     The partial derivatives and constraints confirm that the maximum value of \(P\) is indeed achieved at this point.

Thus, the maximum value of \(P\) is:
<#sep#>human: Suppose you are given two strings a and b. You can apply the following operation any number of times: choose any contiguous substring of a or b, and sort the characters in it in non-descending order. Let f(a, b) the minimum number of operations you have to apply in order to make them equal (or f(a, b) = 1337 if it is impossible to make a and b equal using these operations).

For example: 

  * f(ab, ab) = 0; 
  * f(ba, ab) = 1 (in one operation, we can sort the whole first string); 
  * f(ebcda, ecdba) = 1 (in one operation, we can sort the substring of the second string starting from the 2-nd character and ending with the 4-th character); 
  * f(a, b) = 1337. 



You are given n strings s_1, s_2, ..., s_k having equal length. Calculate ∑ _{i = 1}^{n} ∑_{j = i + 1}^{n} f(s_i, s_j).
Input

The first line contains one integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5) — the number of strings.

Then n lines follow, each line contains one of the strings s_i, consisting of lowercase Latin letters. |s_1| = |s_2| = … = |s_n|, and n ⋅ |s_1| ≤ 2 ⋅ 10^5. All these strings are pairwise distinct.

Output

Print one integer: ∑ _{i = 1}^{n} ∑_{j = i + 1}^{n} f(s_i, s_j).

Examples

Input


4
zzz
bac
abc
acb


Output


4015


Input


2
a
b


Output


1337

+from collections import OrderedDict
+from .core import TaggedEC2Resource
+from ..utils import generic_filter, random_launch_template_id, utc_date_and_time
+from ..exceptions import InvalidLaunchTemplateNameError
+
+
+class LaunchTemplateVersion(object):
+    def __init__(self, template, number, data, description):
+        self.template = template
+        self.number = number
+        self.data = data
+        self.description = description
+        self.create_time = utc_date_and_time()
+
+    @property
+    def image_id(self):
+        return self.data.get("ImageId", "")
+
+    @property
+    def instance_type(self):
+        return self.data.get("InstanceType", "")
+
+    @property
+    def security_groups(self):
+        return self.data.get("SecurityGroups", [])
+
+    @property
+    def user_data(self):
+        return self.data.get("UserData", "")
+
+
+class LaunchTemplate(TaggedEC2Resource):
+    def __init__(self, backend, name, template_data, version_description):
+        self.ec2_backend = backend
+        self.name = name
+        self.id = random_launch_template_id()
+        self.create_time = utc_date_and_time()
+
+        self.versions = []
+        self.create_version(template_data, version_description)
+        self.default_version_number = 1
+
+    def create_version(self, data, description):
+        num = len(self.versions) + 1
+        version = LaunchTemplateVersion(self, num, data, description)
+        self.versions.append(version)
+        return version
+
+    def is_default(self, version):
+        return self.default_version == version.number
+
+    def get_version(self, num):
+        if str(num).lower() == "$latest":
+            return self.versions[-1]
+        if str(num).lower() == "$default":
+            return self.default_version()
+        return self.versions[int(num) - 1]
+
+    def default_version(self):
+        return self.versions[self.default_version_number - 1]
+
+    def latest_version(self):
+        return self.versions[-1]
+
+    @property
+    def latest_version_number(self):
+        return self.latest_version().number
+
+    def get_filter_value(self, filter_name):
+        if filter_name == "launch-template-name":
+            return self.name
+        else:
+            return super().get_filter_value(filter_name, "DescribeLaunchTemplates")
+
+
+class LaunchTemplateBackend(object):
+    def __init__(self):
+        self.launch_template_name_to_ids = {}
+        self.launch_templates = OrderedDict()
+        self.launch_template_insert_order = []
+        super().__init__()
+
+    def create_launch_template(self, name, description, template_data):
+        if name in self.launch_template_name_to_ids:
+            raise InvalidLaunchTemplateNameError()
+        template = LaunchTemplate(self, name, template_data, description)
+        self.launch_templates[template.id] = template
+        self.launch_template_name_to_ids[template.name] = template.id
+        self.launch_template_insert_order.append(template.id)
+        return template
+
+    def get_launch_template(self, template_id):
+        return self.launch_templates[template_id]
+
+    def get_launch_template_by_name(self, name):
+        return self.get_launch_template(self.launch_template_name_to_ids[name])
+
+    def delete_launch_template(self, name, tid):
+        if name:
+            tid = self.launch_template_name_to_ids[name]
+        return self.launch_templates.pop(tid)
+
+    def describe_launch_templates(
+        self, template_names=None, template_ids=None, filters=None
+    ):
+        if template_names and not template_ids:
+            template_ids = []
+            for name in template_names:
+                template_ids.append(self.launch_template_name_to_ids[name])
+
+        if template_ids:
+            templates = [
+                self.launch_templates[tid]
+                for tid in template_ids
+                if tid in self.launch_templates
+            ]
+        else:
+            templates = list(self.launch_templates.values())
+
+        return generic_filter(filters, templates)

+45<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class that models a book with attributes `title`, `author`, `isbn`, and `publication_year`. The class should have an initializer method to set these attributes. Additionally, implement a method `is_classic` that returns `True` if the book was published before 1950, and `False` otherwise. 

You will be given a list of books with their details. For each book, determine if it is a classic and output the result in the format specified below.

**Input:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains four elements: `title`, `author`, `isbn`, and `publication_year`.

**Output:**
- For each book, print a line in the format: `"'<title>' by <author> is a classic: <True/False>"`.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[
    ("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949),
    ("To Kill a Mockingbird", "Harper Lee", "9780060935467", 1960),
    ("Pride and Prejudice", "Jane Austen", "9781503290617", 1813),
    ("The Great Gatsby", "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "9780743273565", 1925)
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```
'1984' by George Orwell is a classic: True
'To Kill a Mockingbird' by Harper Lee is a classic: False
'Pride and Prejudice' by Jane Austen is a classic: True
'The Great Gatsby' by F. Scott Fitzgerald is a classic: True
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, publication_year):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given title, author, isbn, and publication_year.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        :param publication_year: The year the book was published.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.publication_year = publication_year

    def is_classic(self):
        """
        Determines if the book is a classic, defined as being published before 1950.
        
        :return: True if the book is a classic, False otherwise.
        """
        return self.publication_year < 1950

# Creating a list of Book objects
books = [
    Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949),
    Book("To Kill a Mockingbird", "Harper Lee", "9780060935467", 1960),
    Book("Pride and Prejudice", "Jane Austen", "9781503290617", 1813),
    Book("The Great Gatsby", "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "9780743273565", 1925)
]

# Checking if each book is a classic and printing the result
for book in books:
    print(f"'{book.title}' by {book.author} is a classic: {book.is_classic()}")

128<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple slot machine game simulation. The slot machine has three reels, each displaying one of five possible symbols: cherry, banana, lemon, plum, and bell. The symbols are uniformly distributed across all reels. The player begins with 10 coins and bets 1 coin per spin. The payout rules are as follows:
- If all three reels display the same symbol, the player wins 5 additional coins.
- If exactly two reels display the same symbol, the player wins 1 additional coin.
- If no reels display the same symbol, the player wins nothing.

The game continues until the player runs out of coins. For each spin, output the symbols displayed on the reels and the player's remaining coins. Assume that the random number generator is seeded externally, and you do not need to handle input/output operations for the seed.

**Sample Input:**
(No input required as the game is self-contained and relies on random outcomes)

**Sample Output:**
```
Spinning... You have 10 coins.
You got: cherry - banana - lemon
You didn't win this time!
Remaining coins: 9
------------------------
Spinning... You have 9 coins.
You got: plum - plum - banana
You won 1 coin!
Remaining coins: 10
------------------------
Spinning... You have 10 coins.
You got: bell - bell - bell
You won 5 coins!
Remaining coins: 15
------------------------
...
You ran out of coins. Game over!
```

```python
import random

def slot_machine():
    """
    Simulates a simple slot machine game with three reels. The player starts with 10 coins and bets 1 coin per spin.
    The game ends when the player runs out of coins. Outputs the result of each spin and the player's remaining coins.
    """
    symbols = ['cherry', 'banana', 'lemon', 'plum', 'bell']
    coins = 10

    while coins > 0:
        print(f"Spinning... You have {coins} coins.")

        # Spin the reels
        reel1 = random.choice(symbols)
        reel2 = random.choice(symbols)
        reel3 = random.choice(symbols)

        print(f"You got: {reel1} - {reel2} - {reel3}")

        # Deduct one coin for the spin
        coins -= 1

        # Check for winning conditions
        if reel1 == reel2 == reel3:
            print("You won 5 coins!")
            coins += 5
        elif reel1 == reel2 or reel1 == reel3 or reel2 == reel3:
            print("You won 1 coin!")
            coins += 1
        else:
            print("You didn't win this time!")

        print(f"Remaining coins: {coins}")
        print("------------------------")

        # End the game if the player runs out of coins
        if coins <= 0:
            print("You ran out of coins. Game over!")

slot_machine()

</think>To find the derivative of the function \( f(x^n) \) where \( n \) is a positive integer, we use the chain rule. The chain rule states that for a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), the derivative is given by:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} f(g(x)) = f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)
\]

In this problem, let \( u = x^n \). Then the function \( f(x^n) \) can be written as \( f(u) \). According to the chain rule, the derivative of \( f(u) \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} f(u) = f'(u) \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
\]

Next, we need to find \( \frac{du}{dx} \). Since \( u = x^n \), we use the power rule to differentiate \( u \) with respect to \( x \):

\[
\frac{du}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx} (x^n) = n x^{n-1}
\]

Substituting \( \frac{du}{dx} \) back into the chain rule expression, we get:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} f(x^n) = f'(x^n) \cdot n x^{n-1}
\]

Therefore, the derivative of \( f(x^n) \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{f'(x^n) \cdot n x^{n-1}}

The issue is due to line https://github.com/spulec/moto/blob/master/moto/s3/models.py#L1616 which is doing a deep copy of something it should not be deep copying as the structure is complicated with locks and what not.  In certain scenarios the file will be closed causing it to throw an exception.  See https://github.com/spulec/moto/issues/5341#issuecomment-1287429197

go to description<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple file server in Python. The server must be capable of handling multiple clients simultaneously and should allow clients to request and download files from the server. The server should listen for incoming connections on a specified port and respond to client requests as follows:

- **Input:**
  - The server should listen on `127.0.0.1` and port `65432`.
  - Clients will send a filename as a string.
  - The server should have a directory named `server_files/` containing files that can be requested by clients.

- **Output:**
  - If the requested file exists, the server should send the file's contents to the client.
  - If the requested file does not exist, the server should send an error message: `Error: File '<filename>' does not exist on the server.`

- **Sample Interaction:**
  - Client sends: `example.txt`
  - If `example.txt` exists in `server_files/`, the server sends the file's contents.
  - If `example.txt` does not exist, the server sends: `Error: File 'example.txt' does not exist on the server.`

- **Constraints:**
  - The server should handle multiple clients simultaneously.
  - The server should gracefully handle any errors and inform the client of the error.

```python
import socket
import threading
import os

SERVER_IP = '127.0.0.1'
SERVER_PORT = 65432
BUFFER_SIZE = 1024
FILE_DIRECTORY = 'server_files/'

def handle_client(client_socket, client_addr):
    """
    Handles a client connection by receiving a file request and sending the file if it exists.
    
    Args:
        client_socket (socket.socket): The socket object for the client connection.
        client_addr (tuple): The address of the client.
    """
    print(f"Accepted connection from {client_addr}")

    try:
        file_name = client_socket.recv(BUFFER_SIZE).decode()
        print(f"Received request for file: {file_name}")

        file_path = os.path.join(FILE_DIRECTORY, file_name)
        if not os.path.isfile(file_path):
            error_message = f"Error: File '{file_name}' does not exist on the server."
            client_socket.send(error_message.encode())
            print(error_message)
            return

        with open(file_path, 'rb') as file:
            while True:
                data = file.read(BUFFER_SIZE)
                if not data:
                    break
                client_socket.send(data)

        print(f"Successfully sent file '{file_name}' to {client_addr}")

    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error handling client {client_addr}: {e}")

    finally:
        client_socket.close()
        print(f"Closed connection to {client_addr}")

def start_server():
    """
    Starts the file server, listening for incoming connections and handling them in separate threads.
    """
    server_socket = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
    server_socket.bind((SERVER_IP, SERVER_PORT))
    server_socket.listen()
    print(f"Server listening on {SERVER_IP}:{SERVER_PORT}")

    try:
        while True:
            client_sock, client_address = server_socket.accept()
            client_thread = threading.Thread(target=handle_client, args=(client_sock, client_address))
            client_thread.start()

    except KeyboardInterrupt:
        print("Server is shutting down.")

    finally:
        server_socket.close()
        print("Server socket closed.")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    start_server()

Examples: 3628800, 9876543211, 12586269025<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class named `CSVReader` that reads a CSV file and provides methods to access its contents. The CSV file will have a header row, and the class should be able to return the headers, all data rows, a specific row by index, and all values from a specific column by name. Your implementation should handle potential errors such as invalid row indices and non-existent column names.

**Input:**
- A CSV file named `data.csv` with the following content:
  ```
  Name,Age,City
  Alice,30,New York
  Bob,25,Los Angeles
  Charlie,35,Chicago
  ```

**Output:**
- The class should provide the following methods:
  - `get_headers()`: Returns `['Name', 'Age', 'City']`.
  - `get_data()`: Returns `[{'Name': 'Alice', 'Age': '30', 'City': 'New York'}, {'Name': 'Bob', 'Age': '25', 'City': 'Los Angeles'}, {'Name': 'Charlie', 'Age': '35', 'City': 'Chicago'}]`.
  - `get_row(1)`: Returns `{'Name': 'Bob', 'Age': '25', 'City': 'Los Angeles'}`.
  - `get_column('Age')`: Returns `['30', '25', '35']`.

```python
import csv

class CSVReader:
    def __init__(self, filename):
        """
        Initializes the CSVReader with the given filename and reads the CSV file.
        """
        self.filename = filename
        self.headers = []
        self.data = []
        self._read_csv()

    def _read_csv(self):
        """
        Reads the CSV file and populates the headers and data attributes.
        """
        with open(self.filename, mode='r', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as file:
            csv_reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            self.headers = csv_reader.fieldnames
            for row in csv_reader:
                self.data.append(row)

    def get_headers(self):
        """
        Returns the list of headers (column names) from the CSV file.
        
        :return: List of column names.
        """
        return self.headers

    def get_data(self):
        """
        Returns all the data rows from the CSV file.
        
        :return: List of dictionaries, each representing a row.
        """
        return self.data

    def get_row(self, index):
        """
        Returns a specific row by index.
        
        :param index: Index of the row to retrieve.
        :return: Dictionary representing the row.
        :raises IndexError: If the index is out of bounds.
        """
        if index < 0 or index >= len(self.data):
            raise IndexError("Row index out of range")
        return self.data[index]

    def get_column(self, column_name):
        """
        Returns all values for a specific column name.
        
        :param column_name: Name of the column to retrieve.
        :return: List of values in the column.
        :raises KeyError: If the column name is not found.
        """
        if column_name not in self.headers:
            raise KeyError("Column not found")
        return [row[column_name] for row in self.data]

4<#sep#>You are given a CSV file named `employees.csv` containing data about employees. Each row in the file includes the following columns: `Employee ID`, `Name`, `Department`, and `Salary`. Your task is to write a Python program that calculates the total salary for each department and prints the results in the format "Department: Total Salary". The program should handle potential errors gracefully, such as missing files or incorrect data formats.

**Input:**
- A CSV file `employees.csv` with the following structure:
  ```
  Employee ID,Name,Department,Salary
  1,Alice,HR,50000
  2,Bob,Engineering,75000
  3,Charlie,HR,60000
  4,David,Engineering,80000
  5,Eve,Marketing,55000
  ```

**Output:**
- The program should output the total salary for each department:
  ```
  HR: 110000.0
  Engineering: 155000.0
  Marketing: 55000.0
  ```

**Constraints:**
- The CSV file may be missing or contain incorrect data formats. Your program should handle these cases gracefully.

```python
import csv

def calculate_total_salary_per_department(csv_file_path):
    """
    Calculate the total salary for each department from a given CSV file.

    Parameters:
    csv_file_path (str): The path to the CSV file containing employee data.

    Returns:
    None: Prints the total salary for each department.
    """
    department_salaries = {}
    
    try:
        with open(csv_file_path, mode='r', newline='') as csvfile:
            csv_reader = csv.DictReader(csvfile)
            
            required_columns = {'Employee ID', 'Name', 'Department', 'Salary'}
            if not required_columns.issubset(csv_reader.fieldnames):
                raise ValueError(f"The CSV file must contain the following columns: {required_columns}")
            
            for row in csv_reader:
                try:
                    department = row['Department']
                    salary = float(row['Salary'])
                    
                    if department not in department_salaries:
                        department_salaries[department] = 0
                    
                    department_salaries[department] += salary
                
                except ValueError:
                    print(f"Skipping row with invalid data: {row}")
    
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file {csv_file_path} was not found.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {str(e)}")
    
    for department, total_salary in department_salaries.items():
        print(f"{department}: {total_salary}")

# Example usage:
# calculate_total_salary_per_department("employees.csv")

If we take T₃(2t -1) and divide by 32 to make the leading coefficient 1, we get t³ - (48/32)t² + (18/32)t -1/32, which simplifies to t³ - (3/2)t² + (9/16)t - 1/32. Wait, let me check the leading coefficient. If the shifted Chebyshev polynomial scaled to be monic has a maximum of 1/32, then yes. But let's compute this.

Original Chebyshev polynomial on [-1,1]: T₃(x) = 4x³ -3x. When we substitute x = 2t -1, we get 32t³ -48t² +18t -1, as before. To make this monic, we divide by 32, so:

p(t) = t³ - (48/32)t² + (18/32)t -1/32 = t³ - (3/2)t² + (9/16)t - 1/32.

The maximum of |p(t)| on [0,1] is 1/32, since the original T₃(2t -1) had maximum 1, and we divided by 32. So, if this polynomial is indeed the optimal one, then the minimal maximum value is 1/32. Therefore, the answer should be 1/32. But I need to confirm if this is correct.

Wait, but when I compute p(t) at t = 0: p(0) = -1/32.

At t = 1: p(1) = 1 - 3/2 + 9/16 -1/32. Let's compute:

So p(1) = 32/32 - 48/32 + 18/32 -1/32 = (32 -48 +18 -1)/32 = (1)/32. So p(1) = 1/32.

At t where the derivative is zero: p’(t) = 3t² - 3t + 9/16. Let’s find critical points.

Set derivative to zero:

3t² - 3t + 9/16 = 0.

Multiply both sides by 16: 48t² -48t + 9 = 0.

Divide by 3: 16t² -16t +3=0.

Solutions: t = [16 ± sqrt(256 - 192)] / 32 = [16 ± sqrt(64)] /32 = [16 ±8]/32 = 24/32= 3/4 or 8/32=1/4.

So critical points at t=1/4 and t=3/4.

Compute p(1/4):

= 1/64 - 3/32 + 9/64 - 2/64

1/64 - 6/64 + 9/64 - 2/64 = (1 -6 +9 -2)/64 = 2/64 = 1/32.

This site uses Akismet to reduce spam. Learn how your comment data is processed.<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a database schema for a library management system. The system should support multiple libraries, each with its own books, patrons, and transactions. The schema should include tables for `Libraries`, `Books`, `Patrons`, `Transactions`, and any necessary relationships and constraints. Consider the following requirements:

- Each library can have multiple books and multiple patrons.
- Each book can belong to multiple libraries.
- Each patron can have multiple transactions.
- Each transaction involves checking out a book for a patron.

Define the schema using SQLAlchemy ORM. Your schema should be able to handle the following operations:
- Add a new library.
- Add a new book.
- Add a new patron to a library.
- Record a transaction where a patron checks out a book.

**Sample Input:**
```python
# Adding Libraries
library1 = Library(name="Central Library", address="123 Main St")
library2 = Library(name="Downtown Library", address="456 Elm St")

# Adding Books
book1 = Book(title="1984", author="George Orwell", isbn="9780451524935")
book2 = Book(title="To Kill a Mockingbird", author="Harper Lee", isbn="9780060935467")

# Adding Patrons
patron1 = Patron(first_name="John", last_name="Doe", library=library1)
patron2 = Patron(first_name="Jane", last_name="Smith", library=library2)

# Recording Transactions
transaction1 = Transaction(checkout_date="2023-10-01", due_date="2023-10-15", book=book1, patron=patron1)
transaction2 = Transaction(checkout_date="2023-10-02", due_date="2023-10-16", book=book2, patron=patron2)
```

```python
from sqlalchemy import create_engine, Column, Integer, String, ForeignKey, Table
from sqlalchemy.orm import relationship, declarative_base

Base = declarative_base()

# Association table for the many-to-many relationship between Books and Libraries
book_library_association = Table('book_library_assoc', Base.metadata,
                                 Column('book_id', Integer, ForeignKey('books.id'), primary_key=True),
                                 Column('library_id', Integer, ForeignKey('libraries.id'), primary_key=True))


class Library(Base):
    __tablename__ = 'libraries'
    
    id = Column(Integer, primary_key=True)
    name = Column(String, nullable=False, unique=True)
    address = Column(String, nullable=False)
    
    # One to many relationship with Books through an association table
    books = relationship(
        "Book", 
        secondary=book_library_association,
        back_populates="libraries"
    )
    
    # One to many relationship with Patrons
    patrons = relationship("Patron", back_populates="library")
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"<Library({self.name}, {self.address})>"


class Book(Base):
    __tablename__ = 'books'
    
    id = Column(Integer, primary_key=True)
    title = Column(String, nullable=False)
    author = Column(String, nullable=False)
    isbn = Column(String, unique=True, nullable=False)
    
    # Many to many relationship with Libraries
    libraries = relationship(
        "Library", 
        secondary=book_library_association,
        back_populates="books"
    )
    
    # One to many relationship with Transactions
    transactions = relationship("Transaction", back_populates="book")
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"<Book({self.title}, {self.author}, {self.isbn})>"


class Patron(Base):
    __tablename__ = 'patrons'
    
    id = Column(Integer, primary_key=True)
    first_name = Column(String, nullable=False)
    last_name = Column(String, nullable=False)
    library_id = Column(Integer, ForeignKey('libraries.id'))
    
    # Many to one relationship with Library
    library = relationship("Library", back_populates="patrons")
    
    # One to many relationship with Transactions
    transactions = relationship("Transaction", back_populates="patron")
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return f"<Patron({self.first_name} {self.last_name}, Library:{self.library.name})>"


class Transaction(Base):
    __tablename__ = 'transactions'
    
    id = Column(Integer, primary_key=True)
    checkout_date = Column(String, nullable=False)
    due_date = Column(String, nullable=False)
    return_date = Column(String, nullable=True)  # Nullable because a book may not be returned yet
    book_id = Column(Integer, ForeignKey('books.id'))
    patron_id = Column(Integer, ForeignKey('patrons.id'))
    
    # Many to one relationship with Book
    book = relationship("Book", back_populates="transactions")
    
    # Many to one relationship with Patron
    patron = relationship("Patron", back_populates="transactions")
    
    def __repr__(self):
        return (f"<Transaction({self.checkout_date}, {self.due_date}, "
                f"{self.return_date}, Book:{self.book.title}, Patron:{self.patron.first_name} {self.patron.last_name})>")

</think>To find the minimum value of \( f(a, b, c) = \max_{0 \le x \le 1} |x^3 + ax^2 + bx + c| \), we can use the Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind, which minimizes the maximum deviation from zero over an interval. 

First, consider the Chebyshev polynomial \( T_3(x) = 4x^3 - 3x \) on the interval \([-1, 1]\). We transform this polynomial to the interval \([0, 1]\) using the substitution \( x = 2t - 1 \):

\[
T_3(2t - 1) = 4(2t - 1)^3 - 3(2t - 1)
\]

Expanding and simplifying:

\[
(2t - 1)^3 = 8t^3 - 12t^2 + 6t - 1

\[
4(2t - 1)^3 = 32t^3 - 48t^2 + 24t - 4

\]

To make this polynomial monic (leading coefficient 1), we divide by 32:

\[

1. \( p(0) = -\frac{1}{32} \)
2. \( p(1) = 1 - \frac{3}{2} + \frac{9}{16} - \frac{1}{32} = \frac{32}{32} - \frac{48}{32} + \frac{18}{32} - \frac{1}{32} = \frac{1}{32} \)

\[

\[
3t^2 - 3t + \frac{9}{16} = 0

\[

Now the problem is computationally feasible if we can, for each pair, determine if they are compatible in this manner. But even so, for large n (up to 2e5), with a group of e.g. 1e5 elements, the 1e10 pairs is impossible. 

Thus, we need an efficient way to find the number of such pairs in a group.

Hmm, so how can I represent or precompute this?

Possible idea: For each string s_i and s_j in the group, check whether there exists a substring in either s_i or s_j's conversion that can sort to the other.

This feels computationally expensive if done naively.

Alternative idea: for a given pair of strings (s_i, s_j), there exists a substring [l, r] in either s_i or s_j such that when you sort that substring, you get the desired other string.

Therefore, for s_i to s_j, the two options are:

Either:

1. s_j can be obtained by sorting a substring of s_i.

OR 

2. s_i can be obtained by sorting a substring of s_j.

If either is true, then the pair counts towards the 1-step.

So for each pair, we need to check if either of these two possibilities hold.

But again, checking this for every pair would be O(m^2) for the group, which may be too slow.

Hmm. Need a better approach.

Alternative Idea:

For a pair a and b, to see if b can be reached in one operation from a:

What conditions must hold?

It requires that there is some substring [i...j] of a such that, when you sort that substring in non-decreasing order, you get b.

Similarly, you need to check whether there's a substring in b to sort so that you get a. 

So for a pair (a, b), to check if possible via one operation, either:

a contains a window [start, end] when sorted gives exactly b.

OR 

b contains a window when sorted gives exactly a.

Hence, the problem is to count for the pair, the existence of such a window in either direction.

Now, perhaps for a given string a and b, we can find whether any such interval exists.

But doing this for all pairs in a large group may be feasible through some hashing or rolling hash techniques?

Let me think:

Let us first consider the first condition for a to become b via a single operation on a.

This requires that there exists an interval [l, r], such that if we sort the substring a[l..r], the result is exactly b.

To find whether such an interval exists.

Similarly for the vice versa case.

Hmm.

How do I quickly determine this?

Let me think of the following:

Suppose we consider for a given a and b, which are different and have the same character multiset.

We need to find a window [L, R] in a such that the sorted version of a's [L..R] substring equals the corresponding substring in b.

And the remaining parts of the strings must be the same except for that substring.

The entire string is suppose modified by sorting the substring a[L..R] to become b. 

So, the other parts (outside L..R in a) must remain the same as in a. But the part in the window is now sorted, and must match exactly the same part of b.

Wait yes. 

So, to find if such exists:

The following must be true:

There is an interval [L..R] in a such that:

- For all positions not in [L..R], a has same characters as b in those positions.

- For the interval [L..R], when sorted (non-decreasing), the resulting characters exactly match the corresponding part of b's substring.

Therefore, to check:

The substrings outside the interval must already match between a and b. And the substring inside the interval [L..R] in a, when sorted, must match the corresponding substring in b.

Thus, for each possible L and R in a and b:

Check whether the non-LR parts of a and b are the same. 

And the LR part in a, when sorted, equals the LR of b.

Thus, this would be computationally expensive unless we can find a smart way.

Hmm. 

Alternative Idea:

The entire b must be exactly a, with the exception of a single interval which has been sorted. So:

The difference between a and b must lie entirely within that interval. 

Let me consider that for a to find a suitable [L..R], the differing characters between a and b must all be within some interval.

So suppose that the positions where a differs from b are contained within some interval [L, R]. 

Additionally, the sorted interval of a's substring must exactly match b's substring in that interval.

So, the first condition is that all the differing positions between a and b must lie within a single interval [L, R].

If that is true, then check whether the sorted a's substring in that interval equals the mismatched substring in b. 

But how do I determine if there exists some interval containing all the differing positions?

The set of differing indices can be represented as a set D. The minimal interval containing D must have start <= min(D), max(D) >= max(D). So to see if they form a contiguous block?

Not quite. The set D must be contained within some interval of positions. The minimal such interval is [min(D), max(D)]. 

Thus, the first requirement is that all differing positions are within [min(D), max(D)]. Which is correct because that is the minimal interval containing the different positions. 

So, to see if there exists such an interval:

The differing characters between a and b form a range from l to r (so that no differing character exists before l or after r). 

But even if there is such an interval [l..r], the second condition must also hold: that the sorted substring a[l..r] equals the substring b[l..r].

The differing characters between a and and b form a single consecutive interval of positions, and the a's sorted substring in that interval equals the corresponding substring in b.

Additionally, the vice versa condition (checking if a can be made from b) would similarly have to be considered.

Hence, the steps would be:

For all pairs (a, b) in the group:

Check if there exists an interval [L..R] in a where:

all differing characters between a and b are within [L..R], and sorted(a[L..R]) equals the substring of b from L to R.

OR 

the same condition in the other direction (i.e., in b's possible window).

If either applies ⇒ yes, the pair can be done in one step.

Now, how to compute this efficiently?

Let me notice that for each pair a and b:

The differing positions must all be confined in a single contiguous interval.

Let's think of the positions where a and b differ. 

They form some difference set D.

First step: Find the l and r as the min and max of D.

The difference between the a and b outside the interval [l..r] must be zero.

Inside that interval, the sorted a's substring [l..r] must equal b's substring [l..r].

So, to check this:

Compute the set of differing positions between a and b.

If the minimum and maximum of those positions is l and r, and everything outside [l..r] must match, then check whether the sorted a's substring equals the corresponding part in b.

If this is true, then pair qualifies for one step. 

Alternatively, the same check for the reverse (b's sorted interval equals to a's substring).

Wait, no, for the reverse direction (we can also check if there's a window in b which when sorted becomes a).

So for the reverse:

D is the positions where b differs from a. The difference positions same as before.

The check is whether there's a window [L..R] (from b's side) such that when you sort that in b, you get a.

Thus, same as above: compute D, find l, r.

And then check if sorted (b's substring [l..r]) equals a's substring between l and r, and the rest matches. 

Alternatively, since the problem is symmetric, perhaps it's easier to perform the first condition (checks both possibilities).

Wait:

Suppose to decide between a and b if they can be transformed in one step in either way:

Total condition is that either:

Case1 (a -> b in one step) 

OR 

Case2 (b -> a in one step).

### Step 1: How should we make the game?

Well the best way for us to go about making the game would be to lay out the method here. In real life how would we play a game of memory. We'll think of each card as a playing card. We want to customize our images and make it easy to add more at any time, correct? So lets say all the cards constatute a deck. Here's how I recommend we go about starting the game.

1) Shuffle the deck
2) Take out X cards for the deck (a real deck would have 52, and make for a very, very long game. That's why we only use X cards total. This would also make it easy for us to make levels later on, incase we want to change X dynamically.).
3) Photocopy the retreived cards so we have perfect duplication (2 of each card).
4) Shuffle the duplicates (we can't have them be in the same order as our first set).
5) Lastly, deal the cards out from both decks at the same time.

 LOGIC MONSTER! Logic monster sez that ideally you'd want to deal them out randomly because otherwise someone could realize that if the decks were delt at the same time, there is no way N can match N + 2K, thus cutting possibles in HALF! But we'd like to think no one would notice this without knowing how it's programmed. It's not like they actually watched the cards get delt from both decks.

So does that sound like a reasonable form of getting cards and dealing them out? How can we make it more fun?

Now anyone can eventually get all the cards figured out, but it's our job to make the game fun. In that case, we'll keep track of the number of tries and time that occurs during play. This will allow us to get a scoring system, and then the game becomes more competative/interactive.

I'll give this one away gratis, but feel free to check out my free loops and potential upcoming license-mandated ones over in the respective part of Flashkit.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple calculator that supports basic arithmetic operations: addition, subtraction, multiplication, and division. The calculator should repeatedly prompt the user for an operation and two numbers, perform the requested operation, and display the result. The calculator should continue running until the user enters the command "exit". The program should handle invalid inputs gracefully by displaying an appropriate error message and prompting the user again.

**Input:**
- The first line of input is a string representing the operation ("add", "subtract", "multiply", "divide", or "exit").
- If the operation is not "exit", the next two lines are numbers on which the operation should be performed.

**Output:**
- For each operation, output the result of the operation.
- If the operation is "exit", terminate the program.
- If the input is invalid (e.g., non-numeric input for numbers), output "Invalid input! Please enter a number."

**Sample Input:**
```
add
5
3
multiply
2
4
exit
```

**Sample Output:**
```
The result is 8.0
The result is 8.0
Goodbye!
```

```python
def add(x, y):
    """Return the sum of x and y."""
    return x + y

def subtract(x, y):
    """Return the difference of x and y."""
    return x - y

def multiply(x, y):
    """Return the product of x and y."""
    return x * y

def divide(x, y):
    """Return the quotient of x and y, or an error message if y is zero."""
    if y == 0:
        return "Error! Division by zero."
    return x / y

def calculator():
    """Run a simple calculator that performs basic arithmetic operations."""
    while True:
        print("\nOptions:")
        print("Enter 'add' to add two numbers")
        print("Enter 'subtract' to subtract two numbers")
        print("Enter 'multiply' to multiply two numbers")
        print("Enter 'divide' to divide two numbers")
        print("Enter 'exit' to end the program")
        user_input = input(": ").lower()

        if user_input == "exit":
            print("Goodbye!")
            break
        elif user_input in ('add', 'subtract', 'multiply', 'divide'):
            try:
                num1 = float(input("Enter first number: "))
                num2 = float(input("Enter second number: "))
            except ValueError:
                print("Invalid input! Please enter a number.")
                continue

            if user_input == "add":
                print(f"The result is {add(num1, num2)}")
            elif user_input == "subtract":
                print(f"The result is {subtract(num1, num2)}")
            elif user_input == "multiply":
                print(f"The result is {multiply(num1, num2)}")
            elif user_input == "divide":
                print(f"The result is {divide(num1, num2)}")
        else:
            print("Invalid Input!")

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calculator()

\]

\[
t = \frac{72}{96} = \frac{3}{4}, \quad t = \frac{24}{96} = \frac{1}{4}
\]

Evaluating \( p(t) \) at these points:

\[
p\left(\frac{1}{4}\right) = \left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^3 - \frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)^2 + \frac{9}{16}\left(\frac{1}{4}\right) - \frac{1}{32} = \frac{1}{64} - \frac{3}{32} + \frac{9}{64} - \frac{1}{32} = \frac{10}{64} - \frac{8}{32} = \frac{10}{64} - \frac{16}{64} = -\frac{6}{64} = \frac{1}{32}

\[
p\left(\frac{3}{4}\right) = \left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^3 - \frac{3}{2}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)^2 + \frac{9}{16}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right) - \frac{1}{32} = \frac{27}{64} - \frac{27}{32} + \frac{27}{64} - \frac{1}{32} = \frac{54}{64} - \frac{54}{64} = -\frac{1}{32}
\]

patch.m is the correct function to use.

Walter Roberson on 9 Sep 2011
I am unclear as to what your x axis is?
Your sort(c,2) sorts each row independently, which destroys any implicit link you might have of the color in c(2,K) corresponding to the y in c(1,K). Is that what you want? Or were you trying to sort by y value but preserve the color mapped to each y ?
I was thinking that perhaps your x axis is implicitly the column index in to the matrix, but if that is the case then it would not seem right to be sorting the y ?
You effectively have a vector of y values and a vector of corresponding colors, but imagesc() is for rendering 2D matrices colored per-pixel. Is a bar graph perhaps a better representation for your data?
##### 2 CommentsShow 1 older commentHide 1 older comment
Walter Roberson on 9 Sep 2011
If y is a _row_ vector, then bar([y;nan(1,length(y))]) will produce a bar chart in which every bar is colored separately. The color order would, by default, be the color order of the first N entries of the current color map. The bar chart will return an hggroup that has one child patch object per bar (in *this* situation); you could change the FaceColor property or perhaps the CData property in order to set the colors you want.
Note that if you take this approach, you will want to fetch xlim(), subtract 1 from the maximum value, and set that as the new xlim: you would do this because bar() would initially set the xlim to allow a full space for the bar group whose data is all nan.
There are other approaches using bar(), but this way is cleaner.
You could also simulate bar() using line() or plot(), together with fill(), but the way I outline aboe would probably be less effort.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class named `FileAnalyzer` designed to analyze the contents of a text file. The class should support the following functionalities:

- **Initialization**: The constructor `__init__` should accept a single parameter `filename`, representing the path to the file to be analyzed.
- **File Reading**: The method `read_file` should read the entire content of the file and store it internally. Handle potential errors such as file not found or other exceptions gracefully.
- **Line Count**: The method `get_line_count` should return the total number of lines in the file.
- **Word Count**: The method `get_word_count` should return the total number of words in the file.
- **Character Count**: The method `get_char_count` should return the total number of characters in the file.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Given a file `sample.txt` with the following content:
  ```
  Hello World
  This is a test.
  ```
- The expected outputs for the methods are:
  - `get_line_count()` should return `2`.
  - `get_word_count()` should return `6`.
  - `get_char_count()` should return `25` (including spaces and newline characters).

```python
class FileAnalyzer:
    def __init__(self, filename):
        """
        Initializes the FileAnalyzer object with the filename to be analyzed.
        
        :param filename: str, the path to the file to be analyzed.
        """
        self.filename = filename
        self.contents = None

    def read_file(self):
        """
        Reads the contents of the file and stores it in an internal variable.
        Handles file not found and other exceptions.
        """
        try:
            with open(self.filename, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                self.contents = file.read()
        except FileNotFoundError:
            print(f"Error: The file '{self.filename}' was not found.")
            self.contents = ''
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"An error occurred: {e}")
            self.contents = ''

    def get_line_count(self):
        """
        Returns the number of lines in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of lines in the file.
        """
        if self.contents is None:
            print("Error: File contents are not read. Call read_file() first.")
            return 0
        return self.contents.count('\n') + 1 if self.contents else 0

    def get_word_count(self):
        """
        Returns the number of words in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of words in the file.
        """
        if self.contents is None:
            print("Error: File contents are not read. Call read_file() first.")
            return 0
        return len(self.contents.split()) if self.contents else 0

    def get_char_count(self):
        """
        Returns the number of characters in the file.
        
        :return: int, the number of characters in the file.
        """
        if self.contents is None:
            print("Error: File contents are not read. Call read_file() first.")
            return 0
        return len(self.contents) if self.contents else 0

Hmm. 

Let me think of some properties that can be leveraged.

Suppose that for two strings a and b, their difference is exactly one interval, and the difference in that interval can be represented as a sorted block.

Then the following must hold:

Let's compute the set D of differing indices. They must all be between l and r (min to max of D).

Then, the substring of b from l to r must exactly be the sorted substring of a's same segment.

So:

- a[:l-1] == b[:l-1]

- a[r+1:] == b[r+1:]

- sorted(a_l to a_r) == b[l..r].

Similarly for the reverse condition.

To check whether this is true:

We can precompute for each string a, all possible l, r and hash those possible transformed segments. 

But again, computationally intensive.

Hmm, another Idea: the pairs that can be formed in this way are exactly those for which the two strings are "similar" in the sense that their characters are all in the same order except for a contiguous block which is a permutation whose sorted form matches the other string.

Hence, for a pair (a,b), their difference must be a block where in that block, the multiset matches and when sorted forms the part in the other string.

Thus, the number of characters that differ outside any such block is zero.

This suggests that checking for a given a and b requires only a few steps:

1. Find all the differing indices between a and b.

2. Find the min and max of those indices (l and r).

3. Check if outside l..r, the two strings are the same.

4. Check if the sorted version of a's substring[l..r] equals b's substring[l..r], or vice versa.

If any of those conditions holds (either a'ssorted equals b's part, or b'ssorted equals a's corresponding part), then yes, the pair can be done in one.

Thus, for a pair of strings, the process takes O(L) time, where L is the string length.

If L is up to, say, 2e5 (since N* length <=2e5), and the string length per group can be summed up over all groups to that limit.

Thus, for all the pairs in a group, the total time would be O(G * average (L)^2 + total_pairs * L), which for large n and large groups might be too much.

Example: A group with 2e5 strings each of length 100.

Then the number of pairs is 2e5 choose 2 ~ 2e10. Multiply by 100 time steps each → 2e12 operations → impossible even with optimized code.

Thus this approach won't work.

We need to think of another way.

Hmm.

The number of pairs that require a single step can be found by considering for each string a, the number of strings b such that one of the above conditions holds for either a and b or vice versa.

Suppose that for each string, we can compute all possible modified versions, then see how many existing strings are those modified versions. But even that might be too slow.

Alternatively:

The strings which can be formed by a single operation from any string in the group form the possible candidates. Let’s denote the set S of such modified versions for the group. 

The number of pairs is then the sum over all a in the group how many b exist in the group such that b is in S_{a} (the set of strings that a can reach with one operation on itself), plus the similar terms for the reverse.

Subtracting to avoid duplication since when a can reach b via one step from a, it is the same as counted from a or b's perspective.

But even so, the method would require for each string a to generate all possible one-step variants, and then count the number of existing strings in the group that meet this, then divide appropriately. 

But again, for large strings this is compute bound.

## Reporting Bugs

The current implementation of glue.get_table_versions, see [here](https://github.com/getmoto/moto/blob/78840fd71c365bb40214f59a9d7c5f5f3d00b850/moto/glue/responses.py#L73-L85)

it's different from the actual response from boto3. Specifically `VersionId` it's a key of Table, and not a separate Key.
Also UpdateTime seems missing...
A possibility is to do something like that:


```python
return json.dumps(
      {
          "TableVersions": [
            {"Table": {**table.as_dict(version=n), **{"VersionId": str(n + 1), "UpdateTime": ....random time}}
             for n in range(len(table.versions))
                ]
            }
        )
```

A flow rate of 1 cubic foot per minutes. In SI units 4.719474432 x 10-4 cubic meters per second.
1 Acre-Inch per Minute:

- **Initialization**: Create a book with a title, author, publication year, ISBN, and the number of available copies.
- **Borrow Book**: Decrease the number of available copies by one if the book is available. If not, print "The book is not available."
- **Return Book**: Increase the number of available copies by one.
- **Check Availability**: Return `True` if the book has at least one available copy, otherwise return `False`.

Implement the `Book` class with the methods described above. Your implementation should pass the following test cases:

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Input:
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, "978-0451524935", 3)
print(book.is_available())  # True
book.borrow_book()
print(book.available_copies)  # 2
book.return_book()
print(book.available_copies)  # 3
book.borrow_book()
book.borrow_book()
book.borrow_book()
book.borrow_book()  # Output: The book is not available.

Output:
True
2
3
The book is not available.
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, publication_year, isbn, available_copies):
        """
        Initialize a new book with the given details.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param publication_year: The year the book was published.
        :param isbn: The ISBN number of the book.
        :param available_copies: The number of copies available in the library.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.publication_year = publication_year
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.available_copies = available_copies

    def borrow_book(self):
        """
        Borrow a book if available. Otherwise, print a message indicating the book is not available.
        """
        if self.available_copies > 0:
            self.available_copies -= 1
        else:
            print("The book is not available.")

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Return a book, increasing the number of available copies by one.
        """
        self.available_copies += 1

    def is_available(self):
        """
        Check if the book is available.

        :return: True if the book has at least one available copy, otherwise False.
        """
        return self.available_copies > 0

# Example usage:
book_example = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, "978-0451524935", 3)
print(book_example.is_available())  # True
book_example.borrow_book()
print(book_example.available_copies)  # 2
book_example.return_book()
print(book_example.available_copies)  # 3
book_example.borrow_book()
book_example.borrow_book()
book_example.borrow_book()
book_example.borrow_book()  # Output: The book is not available.

+                from moto<#sep#># A radical ideal in a commutative ring is prime if and only if it is not an intersection of two radical ideals properly containing it?

Let $I$ be a radical ideal (i.e. $\sqrt I=I$) in a commutative ring with unity. Then is it true that $I$ is a prime ideal if and only if it is not an intersection of two radical ideals properly containing $I$ (i.e. not an intersection of two properly larger (w.r.t. inclusion) radical ideals ) ?

Yes, it is.

If $I$ is prime, then the conclusion follows easily.

For the converse, write $I=\bigcap_{j\in J} P_j$ with $P_j$ prime ideals. (This can be done since $I$ is radical.) Let's prove that $I$ is prime. Let $ab\in I$ such that $b\notin I$. If $b\notin P_j$ for all $j\in J$, then $a\in I$ and we are done. Otherwise, set $J_1=\{j\in J:b\in P_j\}$. We have $I=I_1\cap I_2$ where $I_1=\bigcap_{j\in J_1} P_j$ and $I_2=\bigcap_{j\in J-J_1} P_j$. Note that $I_1$ and $I_2$ are radical ideals, and moreover $I\subsetneq I_1$ (why?). Since $I$ can not be an intersection of two radical ideals properly containing it, it follows $I=I_2$, so $a\in I$ (why?).

• Why if $I$ is prime , the conclusion follows easily ?
– user228168
May 4, 2015 at 4:53

May 4, 2015 at 14:46
• @SaunDev If $I=I_1\cap I_2$ then $I\supseteq I_1I_2$, so $I=I_1$ or $I=I_2$. (Any more questions?) May 4, 2015 at 20:13<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Matrix` class in Python that supports basic matrix operations such as addition and multiplication. The class should be able to handle matrices of real numbers and should be initialized with a 2D list. Implement the following methods:

- `__init__(self, data)`: Initializes the matrix with the given 2D list `data`.
- `__add__(self, other)`: Implements matrix addition. Raises a `ValueError` if the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
- `__mul__(self, other)`: Implements matrix multiplication. Raises a `ValueError` if the number of columns in the first matrix is not equal to the number of rows in the second matrix.
- `__str__(self)`: Returns a string representation of the matrix for easy printing.

**Input:**
- The input will be provided through the `Matrix` class constructor and the `__add__` and `__mul__` methods.

**Output:**
- The `__str__` method should return a string representation of the matrix, with each row on a new line and elements separated by spaces.

**Sample Input:**
```python
m1 = Matrix([[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]])
m2 = Matrix([[7, 8], [9, 10], [11, 12]])
m3 = m1 + m1
m4 = m1 * m2
```

**Sample Output:**
```
2 4 6
8 10 12
58 64
139 154
```

```python
class Matrix:
    def __init__(self, data):
        """
        Initializes the matrix with the given 2D list `data`.
        
        :param data: A 2D list of real numbers representing the matrix.
        """
        self.data = data
        self.rows = len(data)
        self.cols = len(data[0]) if self.rows > 0 else 0

    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Implements matrix addition.
        
        :param other: Another Matrix instance to be added to this matrix.
        :return: A new Matrix instance representing the sum of the two matrices.
        :raises ValueError: If the matrices do not have the same dimensions.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Matrix) or self.rows != other.rows or self.cols != other.cols:
            raise ValueError("Matrices must be of the same dimensions to add.")
        result = [[self.data[i][j] + other.data[i][j] for j in range(self.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)]
        return Matrix(result)

    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Implements matrix multiplication.
        
        :param other: Another Matrix instance to be multiplied with this matrix.
        :return: A new Matrix instance representing the product of the two matrices.
        :raises ValueError: If the number of columns in the first matrix is not equal to the number of rows in the second matrix.
        """
        if not isinstance(other, Matrix) or self.cols != other.rows:
            raise ValueError("Number of columns in first matrix must be equal to number of rows in second matrix for multiplication.")
        result = [[sum(self.data[i][k] * other.data[k][j] for k in range(self.cols)) for j in range(other.cols)] for i in range(self.rows)]
        return Matrix(result)

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the matrix for easy printing.
        
        :return: A string representation of the matrix.
        """
        return '\n'.join([' '.join(map(str, row)) for row in self.data])

Thor challenge you to solve the following problem:

There are n conveyors arranged in a line numbered with integers from 1 to n from left to right. Each conveyor has a symbol "<" or ">". The initial state of the conveyor i is equal to the i-th character of the string s. There are n+1 holes numbered with integers from 0 to n. The hole 0 is on the left side of the conveyor 1, and for all i ≥ 1 the hole i is on the right side of the conveyor i.

When a ball is on the conveyor i, the ball moves by the next rules:

If the symbol "<" is on the conveyor i, then:

  * If i=1, the ball falls into the hole 0. 
  * If the symbol "<" is on the conveyor i-1, the ball moves to the conveyor i-1. 
  * If the symbol ">" is on the conveyor i-1, the ball falls into the hole i-1. 



If the symbol ">" is on the conveyor i, then:

  * If i=n, the ball falls into the hole n. 
  * If the symbol ">" is on the conveyor i+1, the ball moves to the conveyor i+1. 
  * If the symbol "<" is on the conveyor i+1, the ball falls into the hole i. 



You should answer next q queries, each query is defined by the pair of integers l, r (1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ n): 

  * First, for all conveyors l,l+1,...,r, the symbol "<" changes to ">" and vice versa. These changes remain for the next queries.
  * After that, put one ball on each conveyor l,l+1,...,r. Then, each ball falls into some hole. Find the maximum number of balls in one hole. After the query all balls disappear and don't considered in the next queries.
Input

The first line contains two integers n, q (1 ≤ n ≤ 5 × 10^5 , 1 ≤ q ≤ 10^5).

The second line contains a string s of length n. It consists of characters "<" and ">". 

Next q lines contain the descriptions of the queries, i-th of them contains two integers l, r (1 ≤ l ≤ r ≤ n), describing the i-th query.

Output

Print q lines, in the i-th of them print the answer to the i-th query.

Example

Input


5 6
&gt;&lt;&gt;&gt;&lt;
2 4
3 5
1 5
1 3
2 4
1 5


Output


3
3
5
3
2
3

Note

  * In the first query, the conveyors change to ">><<<". After that, put a ball on each conveyor \{2,3,4\}. All three balls fall into the hole 2. So the answer is 3. 
  * In the second query, the conveyors change to ">>>>>". After that, put a ball on each conveyor \{3,4,5\}. All three balls fall into the hole 5. So the answer is 3. 
  * In the third query, the conveyors change to "<<<<<". After that, put a ball on each conveyor \{1,2,3,4,5\}. All five balls fall into the hole 0. So the answer is 5. 
  * In the fourth query, the conveyors change to ">>><<". After that, put a ball on each conveyor \{1,2,3\}. All three balls fall into the hole 3. So the answer is 3. 
  * In the fifth query, the conveyors change to "><<><". After that, put a ball on each conveyor \{2,3,4\}. Two balls fall into the hole 1, and one ball falls into the hole 4. So, the answer is 2. 
  * In the sixth query, the conveyors change to "<>><>". After that, put a ball on each conveyor \{1,2,3,4,5\}. Three balls fall into the hole 3, one ball falls into the hole 0 and one ball falls into the hole 5. So, the answer is 3.

So when we multiply +4 by -2, we are going to<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a binary search tree (BST) in Python. Your BST should support the following operations:
- Insert a new value into the tree.
- Search for a specific value in the tree.
- Perform an in-order traversal of the tree.
- Find the minimum value in the tree.
- Find the maximum value in the tree.

Implement a class `TreeNode` that represents a node in the BST. The class should have the following methods:
- `insert(key)`: Inserts a new value `key` into the BST.
- `search(key)`: Returns `True` if `key` is found in the BST, otherwise returns `False`.
- `inorder_traversal()`: Returns a list of values in the BST in in-order sequence.
- `find_min()`: Returns the minimum value in the BST.
- `find_max()`: Returns the maximum value in the BST.

**Sample Input:**
```
tree = TreeNode(10)
tree.insert(5)
tree.insert(15)
tree.insert(3)
tree.insert(7)
tree.insert(12)
tree.insert(18)
print(tree.search(7))  # Output: True
print(tree.search(8))  # Output: False
print(tree.inorder_traversal())  # Output: [3, 5, 7, 10, 12, 15, 18]
print(tree.find_min())  # Output: 3
print(tree.find_max())  # Output: 18
```

```python
class TreeNode:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.left = None
        self.right = None
        self.val = key

    def insert(self, key):
        """Inserts a new value into the BST."""
        if self.val is None:
            self.val = key
            return

        if key < self.val:
            if self.left is None:
                self.left = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self.left.insert(key)
        else:
            if self.right is None:
                self.right = TreeNode(key)
            else:
                self.right.insert(key)

    def search(self, key):
        """Returns True if the key is found in the BST, otherwise returns False."""
        if self.val == key:
            return True

        if key > self.val:
            if self.right is None:
                return False
            return self.right.search(key)

        if self.left is None:
            return False
        return self.left.search(key)

    def inorder_traversal(self):
        """Returns a list of values in the BST in in-order sequence."""
        elements = []
        if self.left:
            elements += self.left.inorder_traversal()

        elements.append(self.val)

        if self.right:
            elements += self.right.inorder_traversal()

        return elements

    def find_min(self):
        """Returns the minimum value in the BST."""
        current = self
        while current.left is not None:
            current = current.left

        return current.val

    def find_max(self):
        """Returns the maximum value in the BST."""
        current = self
        while current.right is not None:
            current = current.right

        return current.val
```<#sep#>[(4 +1/16)/(4 +1)] = sqrt[(65/16)/5] = sqrt(13/16) ≈0.9014

         return 200, response_headers, json.dumps(response)
 
+    @amzn_request_id
+    def list_rule_groups(self):
+        scope = self._get_param("Scope")
+        if scope == "CLOUDFRONT":
+            self.region = GLOBAL_REGION
+        rule_groups = self.wafv2_backend.list_rule_groups()
+        response = {"RuleGroups": [rg.to_dict() for rg in rule_groups]}
+        response_headers = {"Content-Type": "application/json"}
+        return 200, response_headers, json.dumps(response)
+
+    @amzn_request_id
+    def list_tags_for_resource(self):
+        arn = self._get_param("ResourceARN")
+        self.region = arn.split(":")[3]
+        tags = self.wafv2_backend.list_tags_for_resource(arn)
+        response = {"TagInfoForResource": {"ResourceARN": arn, "TagList": tags}}
+        response_headers = {"Content-Type": "application/json"}
+        return 200, response_headers, json.dumps(response)
+
+    @amzn_request_id
+    def tag_resource(self):
+        body = json.loads(self.body)
+        arn = body.get("ResourceARN")
+        self.region = arn.split(":")[3]
+        tags = body.get("Tags")
+        self.wafv2_backend.tag_resource(arn, tags)
+        return 200, {}, "{}"
+
+    @amzn_request_id
+    def untag_resource(self):
+        body = json.loads(self.body)
+        arn = body.get("ResourceARN")
+        self.region = arn.split(":")[3]
+        tag_keys = body.get("TagKeys")
+        self.wafv2_backend.untag_resource(arn, tag_keys)
+        return 200, {}, "{}"
+
+    @amzn_request_id
+    def update_web_acl(self):
+        body = json.loads(self.body)
+        name = body.get("Name")
+        _id = body.get("Id")
+        scope = body.get("Scope")
+        if scope == "CLOUDFRONT":
+            self.region = GLOBAL_REGION
+        default_action = body.get("DefaultAction")
+        rules = body.get("Rules")
+        description = body.get("Description")
+        visibility_config = body.get("VisibilityConfig")
+        lock_token = self.wafv2_backend.update_web_acl(
+            name, _id, default_action, rules, description, visibility_config
+        )
+        return 200, {}, json.dumps({"NextLockToken": lock_token})
+

x=11<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Polynomial` class in Python that supports basic arithmetic operations and evaluation. The class should be able to handle polynomials represented by a list of coefficients in descending order of powers. Implement the following methods:

- `__init__(self, coefficients)`: Initializes the polynomial with a list of coefficients.
- `__add__(self, other)`: Adds two polynomials.
- `__sub__(self, other)`: Subtracts one polynomial from another.
- `__mul__(self, other)`: Multiplies two polynomials.
- `evaluate(self, x)`: Evaluates the polynomial at a given value of `x`.

**Sample Input/Output:**

For the polynomials `3x^2 + 2` and `x - 1`:

- Addition: `(3x^2 + 2) + (x - 1) = 3x^2 + x + 1`
- Subtraction: `(3x^2 + 2) - (x - 1) = 3x^2 - x + 3`
- Multiplication: `(3x^2 + 2) * (x - 1) = 3x^3 - 3x^2 + 2x - 2`
- Evaluation at `x=2`: `3x^2 + 2` evaluated at `x=2` is `14`

```python
class Polynomial:
    def __init__(self, coefficients):
        """
        Initializes the polynomial with a list of coefficients.
        Coefficients are in descending order of powers.
        """
        self.coefficients = coefficients
    
    def __add__(self, other):
        """
        Adds two polynomials.
        """
        max_len = max(len(self.coefficients), len(other.coefficients))
        coeffs_self = [0] * (max_len - len(self.coefficients)) + self.coefficients
        coeffs_other = [0] * (max_len - len(other.coefficients)) + other.coefficients
        result = [a + b for a, b in zip(coeffs_self, coeffs_other)]
        return Polynomial(result)
    
    def __sub__(self, other):
        """
        Subtracts one polynomial from another.
        """
        max_len = max(len(self.coefficients), len(other.coefficients))
        coeffs_self = [0] * (max_len - len(self.coefficients)) + self.coefficients
        coeffs_other = [0] * (max_len - len(other.coefficients)) + other.coefficients
        result = [a - b for a, b in zip(coeffs_self, coeffs_other)]
        return Polynomial(result)
    
    def __mul__(self, other):
        """
        Multiplies two polynomials.
        """
        result = [0] * (len(self.coefficients) + len(other.coefficients) - 1)
        for i, coeff1 in enumerate(self.coefficients):
            for j, coeff2 in enumerate(other.coefficients):
                result[i + j] += coeff1 * coeff2
        return Polynomial(result)
    
    def evaluate(self, x):
        """
        Evaluates the polynomial at a given value of x.
        """
        total = 0
        for power, coeff in enumerate(reversed(self.coefficients)):
            total += coeff * (x ** power)
        return total

    def __str__(self):
        terms = []
        for power, coeff in enumerate(reversed(self.coefficients)):
            if coeff == 0: 
                continue
            sign = '+' if coeff >= 0 else '-'
            coeff = abs(coeff)
            if power == 0:
                term = f'{int(coeff)}'
            elif power == 1:
                term = f'{" " if coeff == 1 else f"{int(coeff)}"}x'
            else:
                term = f'{" " if coeff == 1 else f"{int(coeff)}"}x^{power}'
            terms.append(f' {sign} {term}' if terms else (f'{int(coeff)}x^{power}' if power != 0 else f'{int(coeff)}'))
        return ''.join(terms)


# Example usage
p1 = Polynomial([3, 0, 2])  # 3x^2 + 2
p2 = Polynomial([1, -1])    # x - 1

p3 = p1 + p2  # (3x^2 + 2) + (x - 1) = 3x^2 + x + 1
p4 = p1 - p2  # (3x^2 + 2) - (x - 1) = 3x^2 - x + 3
p5 = p1 * p2  # (3x^2 + 2) * (x - 1) = 3x^3 - 3x^2 + 2x - 2

print("p1:", p1)  # Output: 3x^2 + 2
print("p2:", p2)  # Output: x - 1
print("p3:", p3)  # Output: 3x^2 + x + 1
print("p4:", p4)  # Output: 3x^2 - x + 3
print("p5:", p5)  # Output: 3x^3 - 3x^2 + 2x - 2

print("p1 evaluated at x=2:", p1.evaluate(2))  # Output: 14

So after doing this, each ball's destination is straightforward but depends on neighboring directions.

Now, the problem requires for each query:

Flip a range [l, r], so for each conveyor in that interval l to r, their direction is flipped.

After flipping, put a ball on each conveyor in l..r (each of them) and collect all the holes they land in. Then find the maximum count.

The problem is handling this efficiently for multiple queries.

Given that n and q are up to 5e5 and 1e5, respectively, the solution must be O( (n + q) log n ) time or similar. So direct simulation won't work.

So the key is, perhaps, to model for each conveyor:

Given it's current direction, what is its contribution to the hole it falls into? But changes in the conveyor's direction due to flips will require fast updates, and also being able to quickly compute the hole for each conveyor in a range.

Therefore, the problem likely requires a data structure that can track for a given conveyor's direction (which can be toggled), the destination hole, and can compute the maximum frequency in a range of queries (over the quarterbacks being [l..r} with their new direction.

Alternatively, perhaps the destination's "holes" can be represented in a fashion that allows quick queries.

Hmm, but it's not straightforward.

First, perhaps, we can precompute for each conveyor what its destination is when not flipped, then track flip ranges and dynamically compute destinations.

Alternatively, each time a flip occurs, we can track the current direction as a function of the number of flips over ranges. But changing the direction and recomputing for each is O(1) for the flip, but we need the destination.

But to compute the destination, the conveyor's direction depends on its previous state plus all the flip ranges applied so far to include itself.

Wait, maybe the best approach is to model the destination function as a function of the current direction. Since direction can be either flipped or not, perhaps the destination can be determined based only on the direction's current value (flipped or original), and the neighboring conveyor's direction (which may also be flipped or not). 

But neighboring in this case requires information about adjacent conveyors, which complicates things. Since each conveyor's destination depends on the direction of the next/previous conveyors (which also can be affected by prior flips).

This seems difficult to model in a way that can be tracked with a segment tree.

Hmm, maybe it's better to think about the destination for a conveyor's direction and the behavior of neighboring directions. 

But how can the destination be modeled with some properties that can be computed efficiently?

Let me think of the destination as a function:

After flipping some ranges, for each conveyor's current direction (after flips), the destination is determined by its direction and the current state of adjacent conveyors.

Because flipping a range affects the direction of all conveyor in it, this interdependency between adjacent conveyors makes this challenging.

An alternative idea: can each conveyor's destination be determined through some intervals or grouping of conveyors, such that when there's a flip, the groupings can be updated quickly?

<#sep#>Default ACL not set for multipart upload
## Context

When using moto's implementation of S3's multipart upload to upload an object, the ACL seems like it is not set on the object unless it is specified explicitly by the uploader. This causes problems with subsequent requests to access the object, eg. `HeadObject`.

For some reason that I've not got to the bottom of, this issue only seems to occur when using presigned URLs to send the `HeadObject` request, using boto's client methods directly works as expected.

### Expected behaviour

- Upload a large object using multipart upload
- Send a HeadObject request using a presigned URL
- Get a successful response

### Actual behaviour

- Upload a large object using multipart upload
- Send a HeadObject request using a presigned URL
- An `AttributeError` is raised in the `mock_s3` context manager
